\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\log\mathbb{E}[H\text{}C(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},0^{N-r};\lambda_{1}(A),\ldots,\lambda_{N}(A))]=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\Psi(x_{i}),

(63)

where
\Psi
is a smooth function in a neighborhood of
0
and the above
convergence is uniform in a neighborhood of
0^{r}
. Then for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the
k
-th moment of the random measure
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta\left(N^{-1}\lambda_{i}(A)\right)
converges as
N\rightarrow\infty
in
probability to
\Psi^{(k)}(0)/(k-1)!
.

Proof.

First we show that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{k}(A)}{N^{k}}\right]=\frac{\Psi^{(k)}(0)}{(k-1)!}.

(64)

This can be done in a similar way as in Theorem
8
, using that

\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}(A^{k})]=\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\mathcal{D}_{k}f_{N}{\mbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-90.0}{${-\!\!-}$}}}_{x_{i}=i\varepsilon}
, where
f_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}):=\mathbb{E}[H\text{}C(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\lambda_{1}(A),\ldots,\lambda_{N}(A))]
. Unlike condition (
40
), assumption
(
63
) leads to an expansion
N^{k}m_{0,k,N}(\Psi)+N^{k-1}m_{1,k,N}(\Psi)+\cdots
for
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}(A^{k})]
, where
the sequences
(m_{i,k,N}(\Psi))_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converge. This holds
because
\mathcal{D}_{k}f_{N}
is a linear combination of terms

\frac{\left(\partial_{b_{0}}(\log\text{}f_{N})\right)^{l_{1}}\ldots\left(\partial^{k-m}_{b_{0}}\left(\log\text{}f_{N}\right)\right)^{l_{k-m}}}{(x_{b_{0}}-x_{b_{1}})\ldots(x_{b_{0}}-x_{b_{m}})}+\cdots+\frac{(\partial_{b_{m}}(\log\text{}f_{N}))^{l_{1}}\ldots\left(\partial_{b_{m}}^{k-m}(\log\text{}f_{N})\right)^{l_{k-m}}}{(x_{b_{m}}-x_{b_{0}})\ldots(x_{b_{m}}-x_{b_{m-1}})},

(65)

and using the same arguments as before, we obtain that these terms do
not depend on
b_{0},\ldots,b_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and that they converge when

x_{i}=i\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
and
N\rightarrow\infty
. Thus, the limit
\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\mathcal{D}_{k}f_{N}{\mbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-90.0}{${-\!\!-}$}}}_{x_{i}=i\varepsilon}
is a sum, in which the summand with factor
(
65
), for
x_{i}=i\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, will
also have as a factor a polynomial in
N
. Its degree is
m+1
, i.e., the
number of distinct variables in the denominator of (
65
). In
comparison with assumption (
40
), in the context of Theorem
8
in
order to get the similar expansion for
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}(A^{k})]
that
leads to the limit result that we proved, we have to write
f_{N}=\exp\left(N\cdot\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f_{N}\right)
before we compute the
derivatives
\partial_{i}^{k-m}f_{N}
that involved in
\mathcal{D}_{k}f_{N}

according to (
37
). Thus, in that case the corresponding
polynomials in
N
depended also on the differentiation of
f_{N}
. Concluding,
we see that we can get the desirable expansion and the term
N^{k}m_{0,k,N}(\Psi)
will arise from the summand

\frac{k}{\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\partial_{i}f_{N}\partial_{i}^{k-1}\left(\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})\right)

of
\mathcal{D}_{k}f_{N}
. Using the same arguments as before, we obtain
that the terms (
65
) for
b_{0},\ldots,b_{m\in}\{1,\ldots,N\}

will contribute to
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}(A^{k})]
when
m=k-1
,
x_{i}=i\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
with

\binom{N}{k}\binom{k}{k-1}\left(\partial_{1}^{k}(\log\text{}f_{N})(0^{N})+\omicron(1)\right),

which implies that
m_{0,k,N}(\Psi)=(\partial_{1}^{k}(\log\text{}f_{N})(0^{N})+\omicron(1))/(k-1)!
and (
64
) holds. In order to
prove the claim it suffices to show that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
one has

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{k}(A)}{N^{k}}\right]^{2}=\left(\frac{\Psi^{(k)}(0)}{(k-1)!}\right)^{2}.

(66)

We will show that
\frac{1}{N^{2k}}\mathcal{D}_{k}^{2}f_{N}{\mbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-90.0}{${-\!\!-}$}}}_{x_{i}=0}
converges as
N\rightarrow\infty
to the left
hand side of (
66
). The difference compared to Theorem
8

is that now the term
\frac{1}{N^{2k}}\mathcal{D}_{2k}f_{N}{\mbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-90.0}{${-\!\!-}$}}}_{x_{i}=0}
will contribute to the limit
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N^{2k}}\mathcal{D}_{k}^{2}f_{N}{\mbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-90.0}{${-\!\!-}$}}}_{x_{i}=0}
, due
to relation (
64
). Thus, we have to show that

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\left(\frac{1}{N^{2k}\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})}\sum_{\underset{n\neq m}{n,m=1}}^{N}\left.\partial_{n}^{k}\partial_{m}^{k}\left(\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})f_{N}\right)\right|_{x_{i}=0}\right)=\left(\frac{\Psi^{(k)}(0)}{(k-1)!}\right)^{2}-\frac{\Psi^{(2k)}(0)}{(2k-1)!}.

(67)

For the same reasons as in Theorem
8
, the term

\frac{1}{\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})}\sum_{\nu,\mu=0}^{k}\sum_{\underset{n\neq m}{n,m=1}}^{N}\binom{k}{\nu}\binom{k}{\mu}\partial_{n}^{k-\nu}\partial_{m}^{k-\mu}f_{N}\partial_{n}^{\nu}\partial_{m}^{\mu}\left(\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})\right)

(68)

can be controlled when
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\rightarrow 0
if we consider the sum
of specific summands of the form

\binom{k}{\nu}\binom{k}{\mu}\frac{\partial_{a_{0}}^{k-\nu}\partial_{b_{0}}^{k-\mu}f_{N}}{(x_{a_{0}}-x_{a_{1}})\ldots(x_{a_{0}}-x_{a_{\nu}})(x_{b_{0}}-x_{b_{1}})\ldots(x_{b_{0}}-x_{b_{\mu}})}

(69)

in order to make symmetrizations. We recall that for the indices
a_{0},\ldots,a_{\nu},b_{0},\ldots,b_{\mu}
it is required that
a_{0}\neq b_{0}
,

a_{i}\neq a_{j}
,
b_{i}\neq b_{j}
and
|\{a_{0}\}\cap\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{\mu}\}|+|\{b_{0}\}\cap\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{\nu}\}|\leq 1
. Then, writing
f_{N}=\exp(\log\text{}f_{N})
and using the chain rule in order to express the
derivatives
\partial_{a_{0}}^{k-\nu}\partial_{b_{0}}^{k-\mu}f_{N}
,
assumption (
63
) implies that we can get an
expansion
N^{2k}M_{0,k,N}(\Psi)+N^{2k-1}M_{1,k,N}(\Psi)+\cdots
for (
68
), where the sequences
(M_{i,k,N}(\Psi))_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converge. The coefficient of
N^{i}
arises from terms
(
69
) where in the denominator there are at least
i
variables.
The reason that in our expansion there is no summand with factor
N^{2k+2}
is that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
one has

\sum_{\underset{n\neq m}{n,m=1}}^{N}\partial_{n}^{k}\partial_{m}^{k}\left(\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})\right)=-\sum_{n=1}^{N}\partial_{n}^{2k}\left(\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})\right)=0.

(70)

Thus, for
\nu=\mu=k
, the summands (
69
) do not contribute to
(
68
). Similarly, if
(\nu,\mu)=(k-1,k)
and all the indices

a_{0},\ldots,a_{k-1},b_{0},\ldots,b_{k}
are distinct, then the
corresponding terms (
69
) cancel out, since

\frac{k\partial_{a_{0}}f_{N}}{(x_{a_{0}}-x_{a_{1}})\ldots(x_{a_{0}}-x_{a_{k-1}})}\left(\frac{1}{(x_{b_{0}}-x_{b_{1}})\ldots(x_{b_{0}}-x_{b_{k}})}+\cdots+\frac{1}{(x_{b_{k}}-x_{b_{0}})\ldots(x_{b_{k}}-x_{b_{k-1}})}\right)=0.

(71)

Clearly, the same holds if
(\nu,\mu)=(k,k-1)
. That’s why in our
expansion there is no summand with factor
N^{2k+1}
. In order to
determine
M_{0,k,N}(\Psi)
, we have to consider terms of the form (
69
),
where either
(\nu,\mu)\in\{(k-1,k),(k,k-1)\}
and
|\{a_{0},\ldots,a_{\nu},b_{0},\ldots,b_{\mu}\}|=2k
, or
(\nu,\mu)=(k-1,k-1)
and all
a_{0},\ldots,a_{k-1},b_{0},\ldots,b_{k-1}
are
distinct. We start with the first case, where
(\nu,\mu)=(k-1,k)
. We
have that one of the
a_{0},\ldots,a_{k-1}
is equal to one of the
b_{0},\ldots,b_{k}
. If
a_{i}=b_{j}
for some
i\geq 1
and
j\geq 0
, then the
corresponding terms (
69
) cancel out since the equality
(
71
) will also hold in this case. It remains to examine the case
where
b_{i}=a_{0}
, for some
i\geq 1
. Let
\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1},\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{k-1}\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
be fixed and pairwise distinct. Then, for
(\nu,\mu)\in\{(k,k-1),(k-1,k)\}
, we get a contribution

\frac{2kk!(k-1)!\partial_{\alpha_{0}}f_{N}}{(x_{\alpha_{0}}-x_{\alpha_{1}})\ldots(x_{\alpha_{0}}-x_{\alpha_{k-1}})}\left(\frac{1}{(x_{\beta_{0}}-x_{\alpha_{0}})(x_{\beta_{0}}-x_{\beta_{1}})\ldots(x_{\beta_{0}}-x_{\beta_{k-1}})}+\right.

\left.\frac{1}{(x_{\beta_{1}}-x_{\alpha_{0}})(x_{\beta_{1}}-x_{\beta_{0}})\ldots(x_{\beta_{1}}-x_{\beta_{k-1}})}+\cdots+\frac{1}{(x_{\beta_{k-1}}-x_{\alpha_{0}})(x_{\beta_{k-1}}-x_{\beta_{0}})\ldots(x_{\beta_{k-1}}-x_{\beta_{k-2}})}\right)

=\frac{-2kk!(k-1)!\partial_{\alpha_{0}}f_{N}}{(x_{\alpha_{0}}-x_{\alpha_{1}})\ldots(x_{\alpha_{0}}-x_{\alpha_{k-1}})(x_{\alpha_{0}}-x_{\beta_{0}})\ldots(x_{\alpha_{0}}-x_{\beta_{k-1}})}

(72)

to (
68
). The factor
k!(k-1)!
appears in the numerator because
it is the number of different ways that we can differentiate
x_{\alpha_{0}}-x_{\alpha_{1}},\ldots,x_{\alpha_{0}}-x_{\alpha_{k-1}},x_{\beta_{0}}-x_{\alpha_{0}},x_{\beta_{0}}-x_{\beta_{1}},\ldots,x_{\beta_{0}}-x_{\beta_{k-1}}
in
\partial_{\alpha_{0}}^{k-1}\partial_{\beta_{0}}^{k}\left(\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})\right)
in order to get the summand with denominator

(x_{\alpha_{0}}-x_{\alpha_{1}})\ldots(x_{\alpha_{0}}-x_{\alpha_{k-1}})(x_{\beta_{0}}-x_{\alpha_{0}})(x_{\beta_{0}}-x_{\beta_{1}})\ldots(x_{\beta_{0}}-x_{\beta_{k-1}})
when we divide with
\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})
. The
term (
72
) will contribute
\binom{2k-1}{k-1}
times to
(
68
). Indeed, we have to choose
k-1
terms
x_{\alpha_{0}}-x_{i}
,
where
i=\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1},\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{k-1}

to differentiate with respect to
x_{\alpha_{0}}
. Then the terms that we have
to add in order to get (
72
) are fixed. Replacing the
leading variable
x_{\alpha_{0}}
with
x_{\alpha_{1}},\ldots,x_{\alpha_{k-1}},x_{\beta_{0}},\ldots,x_{\beta_{k-1}}
and writing
x_{\alpha_{k+i}}:=x_{\beta_{i}}
for every
i=0,\ldots,k-1
, we have that the
contribution of the terms (
69
), stated above, for the
indices
\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{2k-1}\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, is

\frac{-(2k)!\partial_{\alpha_{0}}f_{N}}{(x_{\alpha_{0}}-x_{\alpha_{1}})\ldots(x_{\alpha_{0}}-x_{\alpha_{2k-1}})}+\cdots+\frac{-(2k)!\partial_{\alpha_{2k-1}}f_{N}}{(x_{\alpha_{2k-1}}-x_{\alpha_{0}})\ldots(x_{\alpha_{2k-1}}-x_{\alpha_{2k-2}})}.

(73)

But when
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\rightarrow 0
and
N\rightarrow\infty
,
(
73
) is equal to

-\frac{(2k)!\Psi^{(2k)}(0)}{(2k-1)!}.

Since we have
\binom{N}{2k}
options for choosing a
2k
-tuple

(\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{2k-1})\in\{1,\ldots,N\}^{2k}
, we deduce
that the contribution of the terms (
69
) to
the left hand side of (
67
) is
-\Psi^{(2k)}(0)/(2k-1)!
.
For the case where
\nu=\mu=k-1
and
\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1},\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{k-1}\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
are all distinct,
we obtain the terms

\frac{2(k!)^{2}\partial_{\alpha_{0}}\partial_{\beta_{0}}f_{N}}{(x_{\alpha_{0}}-x_{\alpha_{1}})\ldots(x_{\alpha_{0}}-x_{\alpha_{k-1}})(x_{\beta_{0}}-x_{\beta_{1}})\ldots(x_{\beta_{0}}-x_{\beta_{k-1}})}.

(74)

The limit of these terms as
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\rightarrow 0
and
N\rightarrow\infty
is

\frac{1}{2}\binom{2k}{k}2(k!)^{2}\lim_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\rightarrow 0}\left(\frac{\Psi^{\prime}(x_{\alpha_{0}})}{(x_{\alpha_{0}}-x_{\alpha_{1}})\ldots(x_{\alpha_{0}}-x_{\alpha_{k-1}})}+\cdots+\frac{\Psi^{\prime}(x_{\alpha_{k-1}})}{(x_{\alpha_{k-1}}-x_{\alpha_{0}})\ldots(x_{\alpha_{k-1}}-x_{\alpha_{k-2}})}\right)

\times\left(\frac{\Psi^{\prime}(x_{\beta_{0}})}{(x_{\beta_{0}}-x_{\beta_{1}})\ldots(x_{\beta_{0}}-x_{\beta_{k-1}})}+\cdots+\frac{\Psi^{\prime}(x_{\beta_{k-1}})}{(x_{\beta_{k-1}}-x_{\beta_{k-2}})}\right)=(2k)!\left(\frac{\Psi^{(k)}(0)}{(k-1)!}\right)^{2}.

The factor
2^{-1}\binom{2k}{k}
exists in the above product because it
is equal to the number of different ways that we can split
(\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1},\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{k-1})
to two
k
-tuples
such that the sum of terms corresponding to the case (
74
) gives the above limit,
as
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\rightarrow 0
and
N\rightarrow\infty
. Hence, the
contribution of terms (
74
) to the left hand side of (
67
)
is
(\Psi^{(k)}(0)/(k-1)!)^{2}
. This implies that (
67
) holds and
the claim has been proven.
∎

Note that the limit regimes of Theorem
8
and Theorem
10

are quite similar. Thus, it is reasonable to ask for an intermediate limit
regime. It is addressed by the next theorem.

Theorem 11

Let
A
be a random Hermitian matrix of size
N

and
0<\theta<1
. Assume that for every finite
r
one has

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N^{\theta}}\log\mathbb{E}[H\text{}C(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},0^{N-r};\lambda_{1}(A),\ldots,\lambda_{N}(A))]=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\Psi(x_{i}),

(75)

where
\Psi
is a smooth function in a complex neighborhood of
0
and the above
convergence is uniform in a complex neighborhood of
0^{r}
. Then, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the
k
-th moment of the random measure
N^{-\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta\left(N^{-1}\lambda_{i}(A)\right)
converges, as
N\rightarrow\infty
, in
probability to
\Psi^{(k)}(0)/(k-1)!
.

Proof.

First, in order to show that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N^{\theta+k}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}^{k}(A)\right]=\frac{\Psi^{(k)}(0)}{(k-1)!},

(76)

we use the same technique as in Theorem
8
and Theorem

10
which gives us an expansion of
\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\mathcal{D}_{k}f_{N}{\mbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-90.0}{${-\!\!-}$}}}_{x_{i}=i\varepsilon}
. In this expansion the leading summand will
be of the form
N^{\theta+k}m_{0,k,N}(\Psi)
, where the sequence
(m_{0,k,N}(\Psi))_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges and the remaining summands will be

\omicron(N^{\theta+k})
. This can be done if we use the formula
(
58
) for the derivatives
\partial_{i}^{k-m}f_{N}
of
f_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})=\mathbb{E}[H\text{}C(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\lambda_{1}(A),\ldots,\lambda_{N}(A))]
, where now
l
is replaced by

\theta
, since (
75
) and Lemma
3
imply that
the terms (
59
) converge as
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\rightarrow 0

and
N\rightarrow\infty
. Thus, for every
m=0,\ldots,k-1
, we can
write the summand

\frac{1}{\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\binom{k}{m}\partial_{i}^{k-m}f_{N}\left.\partial_{i}^{m}\left(\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})\right)\right|_{x_{i}=0}

of
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}(A^{k})]
as a sum of terms of the form
N^{i_{m,k}\theta+j_{m,k}}h_{m,k,N}(\Psi)
, where
(h_{m,k,N}(\Psi))_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

converges. The summand that corresponds to the largest power of
N
is

N^{(k-m)\theta+m+1}\left(\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m)!}\partial_{1}^{m}\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N^{\theta}}\log\text{}f_{N}\right)\right)^{k-m}(0^{N})+\omicron(1)\right).

Since
(k-m)\theta+m+1\leq\theta+k
, for every
m=0,\ldots,k-1
, we deduce that (
76
) holds. On the other hand, in order
to prove

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N^{2\theta+2k}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}^{k}(A)\right]^{2}=\left(\frac{\Psi^{(k)}(0)}{(k-1)!}\right)^{2},

(77)

first note that
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N^{2\theta+2k}}\mathcal{D}_{2k}f_{N}{\mbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-90.0}{${-\!\!-}$}}}_{x_{i}=0}
does not contribute to the
above limit. Similarly, writing

f_{N}=\exp\left(N^{\theta}\cdot\frac{1}{N^{\theta}}\log\text{}f_{N}\right)

and using Lemma

3
and (
75
), the expression

\left.\frac{1}{\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})}\sum_{\underset{n\neq m}{n,m=1}}^{N}\binom{k}{\nu}\binom{k}{\mu}\partial_{n}^{k-\nu}\partial_{m}^{k-\mu}f_{N}\partial_{n}^{\nu}\partial_{m}^{\mu}\left(\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})\right)\right|_{x_{i}=0}

(78)

can be written as a sum of terms of the form
N^{i_{\nu,\mu,k}\theta+j_{\nu,\mu,k}}h_{\nu,\mu,k,N}(\Psi)
, where
(h_{\nu,\mu,k,N}(\Psi))_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges. The largest power of
N
that appears as factor of a
summand is
N^{(2k-\nu-\mu)\theta+\nu+\mu+2}
. In Theorem

10
we have shown that for
(\nu,\mu)=(k-1,k)
the largest
power of
N
that appears as factor of a summand is
N^{\theta+2k}
.
Moreover, we have shown that for
(\nu,\mu)=(k-1,k-1)
,
(
78
) divided by
N^{2\theta+2k}
converges to
(\Psi^{(k)}(0)/(k-1)!)^{2}
, as
N\rightarrow\infty
. Thus, due to
(
70
) and the condition
(2k-\mu-\nu)\theta+\nu+\mu+2\leq 2\theta+2k
, for every
0\leq\nu,\mu\leq k-1
, we deduce that
(
77
) holds.
∎

Remark 2

Comparing Theorems
8
and
10
, we see that they produce Law of Large Numbers results for a matrix
A
in two different regimes of growth. The first one is strongly related with free probability, and it is natural to ask whether the second one is related with it as well. The answer is positive. We show that the regime of growth from Theorem
10
is related to
infinitesimal freeness
.

From the point of view of infinitesimal free probability, Theorem
10
implies
that the empirical spectral distribution of
A/N
converges to
\delta(0)

and the
1/N
correction
\{m_{k}^{\prime}\}
is equal to

m_{k}^{\prime}=\frac{\Psi^{(k)}(0)}{(k-1)!}=\sum_{\pi\in\operatorname{NC}(k)}\sum_{V\in\pi}\frac{\Psi^{(|V|)}(0)}{(|V|-1)!}\prod_{\underset{W\neq V}{W\in\pi}}\kappa_{|W|}(\delta(0)),

where
\{\kappa_{n}(\delta(0))\}
are the free cumulants of
\delta(0)
. In other
words,
\Psi^{\prime}
is the infinitesimal
R
-transform. In Theorem
12
below, we combine the limit regimes of Theorems
8
and
10
(thus, generalizing both of them).

5
Infinitesimal free probability

The goal of this section is to study the first order correction to the Law of Large Numbers for the empirical measure of random matrices via a more detailed information about their Harish-Chandra transform.

Theorem 12

Let
A
be a random Hermitian matrix of size
N
. Assume that for every finite
r
one has

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{E}[H\text{}C(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},0^{N-r};\lambda_{1}(A),\ldots,\lambda_{N}(A))]-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\Psi(x_{i})\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\Phi(x_{i}),

(79)

where
\Psi,\Phi
are smooth functions in a (complex) neighborhood of
0
and the
above convergence is uniform in a (complex) neighborhood of
0^{r}
. Then one has the following limit:

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N\left(\frac{1}{N^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}^{k}(A)\right]-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}\right)=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{k},

(80)

where
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are given by (
54
) and

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{k}:=\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-1}\Phi^{\prime}(x))\right|_{x=0}.

(81)

Proof.

Note that (
79
) implies that the relation (
40
) is satisfied
and the empirical distribution of
A/N
converges in probability to a
measure with moments
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. In the proof of Theorem
8
we have
shown the existence of an expansion

\frac{1}{N^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}^{k}(A)\right]=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\frac{1}{N^{i}}M_{i,N}(\Psi),

(82)

where for every
i\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
the sequence
(M_{i,N}(\Psi))_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges and
M_{0,N}(\Psi)
is a linear combination of
derivatives

\partial_{i_{m}}\ldots\partial_{i_{1}}\left(\partial_{i_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{k-m}(0^{N}),

(83)

where
f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})=\mathbb{E}(H\text{}C(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\lambda_{1}(A),\ldots,\lambda_{N}(A)))
and
m\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
. Due to
(
79
), if
|\{i_{0},\ldots,i_{m}\}|
¿1, then the terms (
83
)
multiplied by
N
will not contribute to the
1/N
correction as
N\rightarrow\infty
. Thus,

N(M_{0,N}(\Psi)-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k})=

=\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m)!}N\left(\partial_{1}^{m}\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{k-m}(0^{N})-\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}(\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m}\right|_{x=0}\right)+\omicron(1),

as
N\rightarrow\infty
. For
m\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}
, applying
Leibniz rule, we have

N\left(\partial_{1}^{m}\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{k-m}(0^{N})-\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}(\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m}\right|_{x=0}\right)=

=\sum_{l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}=m}\frac{m!}{l_{1}!\ldots l_{k-m}!}N\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k-m}\partial_{1}^{l_{i}+1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)(0^{N})-\prod_{i=1}^{k-m}\Psi^{(l_{i}+1)}(0)\right).

(84)

For any choice of
l_{1},\ldots,l_{k-m}
, due to (
79
), we have

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N\left(\partial_{1}^{l_{i}+1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)(0^{N})-\Psi^{(l_{i}+1)}(0)\right)=\Phi^{(l_{i}+1)}(0),\text{\quad for every \ }i=1,\ldots,k-m,

and by induction on
k-m

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k-m}\partial_{1}^{l_{i}+1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)(0^{N})-\prod_{i=1}^{k-m}\Psi^{(l_{i}+1)}(0)\right)=

=\Phi^{(l_{1}+1)}(0)\Psi^{(l_{2}+1)}(0)\ldots\Psi^{(l_{k-m}+1)}(0)+\cdots+\Psi^{(l_{1}+1)}(0)\ldots\Psi^{(l_{k-m-1}+1)}(0)\Phi^{(l_{k-m}+1)}(0).

Thus, as
N\rightarrow\infty
, the expression (
84
) is equal to

(k-m)\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-1}\Phi^{\prime}(x))\right|_{x=0},

which implies that
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N(M_{0,N}(\Psi)-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k})=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{k}
. As a corollary, the claim holds
if
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}M_{1,N}(\Psi)=0
. In order to determine

M_{1,N}(\Psi)
we have to better understand the rule that gives expansion
(
82
) or equivalently how the operator
\mathcal{D}_{k}
acts on
function
f
. Our approach from Theorem
8
shows that the dependence of

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\mathcal{D}_{k}f{\mbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-90.0}{${-\!\!-}$}}}_{x_{i}=i\varepsilon}
on
N
emerges from powers of
N
and the terms
(M_{i,N}(\Psi))_{i=0}^{k}.
The powers of
N
appear from the differentiation of
f=\exp\left(N\cdot\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)
(see formula
(
44
)) and the fact that the symmetric terms (
45
) do not
depend on
b_{0},\ldots,b_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, where
x_{i}=i\varepsilon
, for
i=1,\ldots,N
, and
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.
Thus,
\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\mathcal{D}_{k}f{\mbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-90.0}{${-\!\!-}$}}}_{x_{i=i\varepsilon}}
can be written as a linear combination of terms

N^{l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}}\binom{N}{m+1}c_{N}(m),

(85)

where
m\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
,
l_{1}+2l_{2}+\cdots+(k-m)l_{k-m}=k-m
and
c_{N}(m)
are linear combinations of derivatives of the form
(
49
). In order to determine
M_{1,N}(\Psi)
, we have to compute the
summand with factor
N^{k}
. There are two cases that we have to consider. For

l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}=k-m-1
, i.e.
l_{1}=k-m-2
,
l_{2}=1

and
l_{3}=\cdots=l_{k-m}=0
, the summands of the form (
85
)
with factor
N^{k}
are

N^{k}\left(\frac{k!}{2m!(m+1)!(k-m-2)!}\partial_{1}^{m}\left(\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{k-m-2}\partial_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)(0^{N})+\omicron(1)\right),

where
m\in\{0,\ldots,k-2\}
. On the other hand, for
l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}=k-m
, i.e.
l_{1}=k-m
and
l_{2}=\cdots=l_{k-m}=0
,
we also obtain appropriate summands of the form (
85
). These are

-N^{k}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}i\right)\left(\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m)!}\partial_{1}^{m}\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{k-m}(0^{N})+\omicron(1)\right)

\text{{\hskip 100.00015pt}}=-N^{k}\left(\frac{k!}{2(m-1)!m!(k-m)!}\partial_{1}^{m}\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{k-m}(0^{N})+\omicron(1)\right),

where
m\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}
. For
l_{1}+\ldots l_{k-m}<k-m-1

we obtain summands which have powers of
N
of lower degree. By
the above, we deduce that

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}M_{1,N}(\Psi)=\sum_{m=0}^{k-2}\frac{k!}{2m!(m+1)!(k-m-2)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-2}\Psi^{\prime\prime}(x))\right|_{x=0}

\text{{\hskip 130.0002pt}}-\sum_{m=1}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{2(m-1)!m!(k-m)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m})\right|_{x=0}=0.

This proves the claim.
∎

Remark 3

Ergodic unitarily invariant matrices are included in the context of Theorem

12
. An example is a
Hermitian random matrix
A
of size
N
that satisfies the
relation

\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(\operatorname{Tr}(H\text{}A))\right]=\prod_{b\in\operatorname{Spec}(H)}\exp(N\Psi(b)+\Phi(b)),\text{\quad for every}\ H\in H(N).

(86)

In that case we have

\frac{1}{N}\left(\log\mathbb{E}[H\text{}C(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\lambda_{1}(A),\ldots,\lambda_{N}(A))]-N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Psi(x_{i})\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Phi(x_{i}).

In comparison with a more general setting of Theorem
12
, these matrices
provide the simplest case since they give an expansion

\frac{1}{N^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}^{k}(A)\right]=\sum_{i=0}^{k}m_{i}\frac{1}{N^{i}},

where
m_{0},\ldots,m_{k}
do not depend from
N
. The numbers
m_{0},m_{1}
are
given by (
54
) and (
81
) respectively. Thus, for GUE and Wishart
matrices the
1/N
correction will be equal to zero.

In the context of Theorem
12
, we saw that function
\Psi^{\prime}
plays a crucial role
in the determination of the limit measure, since it gives us the

R
-transform. Now we would like to understand the role of function
\Phi

in the
1/N
correction. This role is quite similar from the
infinitesimal free probability side, in the following sense: For a random
matrix
A
that satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
12
, we consider the
sequence of non-commutative probability spaces
(\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle,\varphi_{N})
, where

\varphi_{N}(P):=\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}(P(N^{-1}A))],\text{\quad for every}\ P\in\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle.

(87)

We also define a non-commutative probability space
(\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle,\varphi)
such that

\varphi(P)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}P(t)\boldsymbol{\mu}(d\text{}t),\text{\quad for every}\ P\in\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle,

(88)

where
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is the probability measure with moments given by
(
54
). Theorem
12
implies the relation

\varphi_{N}(P)=\varphi(P)+\frac{1}{N}\varphi^{\prime}(P)+\omicron(N^{-1}),

(89)

where
\varphi^{\prime}
is a linear functional which sends the identity of

\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle
to zero and
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{k})
are given by (
81
). The free cumulants

(\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{n}(\mathbf{x},\ldots,\mathbf{x}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of

(\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle,\varphi)
are given by

\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{n}(\mathbf{x},\ldots,\mathbf{x})=\frac{\Psi^{(n)}(0)}{(n-1)!},\text{\quad for every}\ n\in\mathbb{N}.

(90)

Similarly, function
\Phi
allows us to compute explicitly the infinitesimal
free cumulants
(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}_{n}(\mathbf{x},\ldots,\mathbf{x}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
(\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle,\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})
. In the next lemma we show that

\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}_{n}(\mathbf{x},\ldots,\mathbf{x})=\frac{\Phi^{(n)}(0)}{(n-1)!},\text{\quad for every}\ n\in\mathbb{N}.

(91)

Lemma 5

Let
\Psi,\Phi
be two infinitely differentiable at 0 functions
and consider sequences
(c_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(c^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, where

c_{n}:=\frac{\Psi^{(n)}(0)}{(n-1)!}\text{\hskip 10.22217ptand\hskip 10.22217pt}c^{\prime}_{n}:=\frac{\Phi^{(n)}(0)}{(n-1)!},\text{\hskip 10.22217ptfor
every \ }n\in\mathbb{N}.

Then we have

\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-1}\Phi^{\prime}(x))\right|_{x=0}=\sum_{\pi\in\operatorname{NC}(k)}\sum_{V\in\pi}c^{\prime}_{|V|}\prod_{W\in\pi;W\neq V}c_{|W|}.

(92)

Proof.

By Leibniz rule we have that the left hand side of (
92
) is equal to

\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\sum_{l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}=m}\frac{k!}{(m+1)!(k-m-1)!\prod_{i=1}^{k-m}l_{i}!}\prod_{i=1}^{k-m-1}\Psi^{(l_{i}+1)}(0)\Phi^{(l_{k-m}+1)}(0).

(93)

We will show that the correspondence that we made in the proof of Lemma
4

between (
k-m
) -tuples
(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k-m})
and non-crossing
partitions
\pi\in\operatorname{NC}(k)
allows us to prove the claim. For fixed
m\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}
, consider non-negative integers
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k-m}
such that
\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{k-m}=m
, and assume that
\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k-m}\}=\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{a}\}
, where
\nu_{i}\neq\nu_{j}
, for every
i\neq j
. We also
define for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,a\}
the quantity

r_{i}:=|\{j=1,\ldots,k-m:\lambda_{j}=\nu_{i}\}|,

which implies that

r_{1}\nu_{1}+\cdots+r_{a}\nu_{a}=m\text{\quad and\quad}r_{1}+\cdots+r_{a}=k-m.

For fixed
j\in\{1,\ldots,a\}
, the number of times that the term

\frac{k!}{(m+1)!(k-m-1)!\prod_{i=1}^{k-m}\lambda_{i}!}\Phi^{(\nu_{j}+1)}(0)(\Psi^{(\nu_{j}+1)}(0))^{r_{j}-1}\prod_{\underset{i\neq j}{i=1}}^{a}(\Psi^{(\nu_{i}+1)}(0))^{r_{i}}

will contribute to the latter sum of (
93
) is equal to the number of
different ways that we can cover
k-m-1
points on a line segment with

r_{1}
elements
\nu_{1}
, …,
r_{j-1}

elements
\nu_{j-1}
,
r_{j}-1
elements
\nu_{j}
,
r_{j+1}

elements
\nu_{j+1}
, …,
r_{a}
elements

\nu_{a}
. This is equal to

\frac{(k-m-1)!}{r_{1}!\ldots r_{j-1}!(r_{j}-1)!r_{j+1}!\ldots r_{a}!}.

Thus, we see that the
(k-m)
-tuple
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k-m})

will contribute to the latter sum of (
93
) a term

\frac{k!}{(m+1)!\prod_{j=1}^{a}(\nu_{j}!)^{r_{j}}}\sum_{i=1}^{a}\frac{\Phi^{(\nu_{i}+1)}(0)(\Psi^{(\nu_{i}+1)}(0))^{r_{i}-1}}{r_{1}!\ldots r_{i-1}!(r_{i}-1)!r_{i+1}!\ldots r_{a}!}\prod_{\underset{j\neq i}{j=1}}^{a}(\Psi^{(\nu_{j}+1)}(0))^{r_{j}}

\text{}=\frac{k!}{(m+1)!\prod_{i=1}^{a}r_{i}!}\sum_{i=1}^{a}r_{i}c^{\prime}_{\nu_{i}+1}(c_{\nu_{i}+1})^{r_{i}-1}\prod_{\underset{j\neq i}{j=1}}^{a}(c_{\nu_{j}+1})^{r_{j}}.

Let
\pi=\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{k-m}\}\in\operatorname{NC}(k)
be a partition
which have
r_{1}
blocks with
\nu_{1}+1
elements,…,
r_{a}
blocks
with
\nu_{a}+1
elements. Such a partition gives a following contribution to the
right hand side of (
92
)

\sum_{i=1}^{k-m}c^{\prime}_{|V_{i}|}\prod_{\underset{j\neq i}{j=1}}^{k-m}c_{|V_{j}|}=\sum_{i=1}^{a}r_{i}c^{\prime}_{\nu_{i}+1}(c_{\nu_{i}+1})^{r_{i}-1}\prod_{\underset{j\neq i}{j=1}}^{a}(c_{\nu_{j}+1})^{r_{j}}.

Since the number of such partitions is equal to
\left((m+1)!\prod_{i=1}^{a}r_{i}!\right)^{-1}k!
, the claim has been proven.
∎

In the rest of this section we will focus on specific examples of random
matrix models, which will allow us to better understand the functional

\varphi^{\prime}
defined in (
89
). Our goal is to compute the signed
measure on
\mathbb{R}
that determines it in the same way that the
functional
\varphi
is determined by relation (
88
) from the
probability measure
\boldsymbol{\mu}
.

Example 2

We consider the Hermitian random matrix of size
N
,
A=N\text{}\cdot A_{1}+\sqrt{N}\cdot A_{2}
, where
A_{1},A_{2}
are GUE matrices.
We also assume that
A_{1},A_{2}
are independent. Then we have
for every
H\in H(N)
,

\mathbb{E}\exp(\operatorname{Tr}\left(H\text{}A\right))=\prod_{x\in\operatorname{Spec}(H)}\exp\left(N\frac{x^{2}}{2}+\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right),

which implies that the
1/N
correction of the matrix
A_{1}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}A_{2}
is given by

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N\left(\frac{1}{N^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}^{k}(A)\right)-\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}(x^{k-m})\right|_{x=0}\right)

=\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}(x^{k-m})\right|_{x=0}.

Using Cauchy formula, we deduce that

\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}(x^{k-m})\right|_{x=0}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\binom{k}{m+1}\oint_{|z|=1}\frac{z^{k-m}}{z^{m+1}}d\text{}z\\
=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}z^{k+1}\sum_{m=1}^{k}\binom{k}{m}\left(\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right)^{m}d\text{}z=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}z^{k+1}\left(\left(1+\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right)^{k}-1\right)d\text{}z=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}z\left(z+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k}d\text{}z.

Using the change of variables
u=2\cos\text{}t
in order to compute the
last contour integral, we obtain the formula

\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}(x^{k-m})\right|_{x=0}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}u^{k}\frac{u^{2}-2}{\sqrt{4-u^{2}}}d\text{}u,

where

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}(d\text{}u)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\boldsymbol{1}_{(-2,2)}(u)\frac{u^{2}-2}{\sqrt{4-u^{2}}}d\text{}u

is a signed measure of total mass
0
.

Example 3

We consider the Hermitian random matrix of size
N
,
A=N\cdot A_{1}+\sqrt{N}\cdot A_{2}
, where
A_{1}=\frac{1}{N}X\text{}X^{\ast}
is a
Wishart matrix. More precisely,
X
is a
N\times M
matrix with
independent and standard complex Gaussian entries. We also assume that
A_{2}

is a GUE matrix and
A_{1},A_{2}
are independent. Then, for every
H\in H(N)
, we have

\mathbb{E}[\exp(\operatorname{Tr}(H\text{}A))]=\exp\left(M\sum_{x\in\operatorname{Spec}(H)}\log(1-x)^{-1}+\sum_{x\in\operatorname{Spec}(H)}\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right),

which implies that the
1/N
correction of the matrix
A_{1}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}A_{2}
is given by

\lim_{M,N\rightarrow\infty\text{, }M-\lambda N\to 0}N\left(\frac{1}{N^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}^{k}(A)\right]-\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}\left(\frac{\lambda}{1-x}\right)^{k-m}\right|_{x=0}\right)

=\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}\left(\left(\frac{\lambda}{1-x}\right)^{k-m-1}x\right)\right|_{x=0}.

(94)

Similarly with the previous example, we calculate the correction measure.
Using that

\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}\left(\left(\frac{1}{1-x}\right)^{k-m-1}x\right)\right|_{x=0}

=\sum_{n=0}^{m}\binom{m}{n}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}}{\mathrm{d}x^{n}}\left(\frac{1}{1-x}\right)^{k-m-1}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m-n}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m-n}}(x)\right|_{x=0}=\binom{m}{m-1}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m-1}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m-1}}(1+x)^{k-3}\right|_{x=0},

for
m\geq 1
and
k\geq 3
, we obtain that (
94
) is equal to

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{m=1}^{k-1}\lambda^{k-m-1}\binom{k}{m+1}\oint_{|z|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}}\frac{(1+z)^{k-3}}{z^{m}}d\text{}z=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}}z(1+z)^{k-3}\lambda^{k}\sum_{m=2}^{k}\binom{k}{m}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda z}\right)^{m}d\text{}z

=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}}\frac{z}{(1+z)^{3}}\left(\lambda+1+\lambda z+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k}d\text{}z

for every
k\geq 3
. This implies that the corresponding linear functional

\varphi_{\lambda}^{\prime}
, defined in (
89
), can be described by the
measure

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(d\text{}u)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\boldsymbol{1}_{(\lambda+1-2\sqrt{\lambda},\lambda+1+2\sqrt{\lambda})}(u)\frac{\lambda u^{2}-2\lambda^{2}u+(\lambda+1)u+(\lambda-1)^{3}}{u^{3}\sqrt{(\lambda+1+2\sqrt{\lambda}-u)(u-\lambda-1+2\sqrt{\lambda})}}d\text{}u.

(95)

More precisely, for
\lambda>1
, we have for every
P\in\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle

\varphi^{\prime}_{\lambda}(P)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}P(t)\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(d\text{}t).

For
\lambda\leq 1
comparing the
k
-th moment of

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{\lambda}
with (
94
), for
k=0,1,2
, we see
that we also have to add derivatives of
\delta\left(0\right)
, in order to obtain a
formula for
\varphi^{\prime}_{\lambda}
that will also give
\varphi^{\prime}_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{1}),\varphi^{\prime}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})
and
\varphi^{\prime}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}^{2})
. Note that for
\lambda=1
the total mass of

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{\lambda}
is infinity.

Other examples of interest are finite-rank perturbations of certain random
matrices. It has been shown that these random matrix models fit in the
framework of infinitesimal free probability, see
[
Sh
]
. In parallel with the
previous works in this direction, we study finite-rank pertubations of ergodic
unitarily invariant matrices, incorporating them into the context of Theorem

12
. For this purpose, we need the following result about their Harish-Chandra integral, which is due to
[
OV
]
.

Lemma 6

Let
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{l}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
l
is fixed. Then the Harish-Chandra integral satisfies, for every fixed
r\in\mathbb{N}
,

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\log\text{}H\text{}C(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},0^{N-r};N\theta_{1},\ldots,N\theta_{l},0^{N-l})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{j=1}^{l}\log(1-\theta_{j}x_{i})^{-1},

where the above convergence is uniform in a neighborhood of
0^{r}
.

The previous lemma allows to obtain explicit formulas for the
1/N

correction of certain random matrices
A
that satisfy the relation
(
40
). More precisely, considering
E_{i,j}
to be the matrix with
unit in the
(i,j)
-th coordinate and all the other entries zero, Theorem
12

implies for the matrix
B=A+\sum_{i=1}^{l}N\theta_{i}E_{i,i}
one has

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N^{k+1}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}^{k}(B)\right]-\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}(\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m}\right|_{x=0}\right)

=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}\left((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-1}\frac{\theta_{i}}{1-\theta_{i}x}\right)\right|_{x=0},

(96)

where
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{l}\in\mathbb{R}
. We examine below specific
cases for the random matrix
A
that allow to relate (
96
) with moments of a
signed measure. As we see in the examples below, the terms
(
96
) are explicit enough in order to determine the corresponding
signed measure for finite rank perturbations of ergodic unitarily invariant
matrices.

Example 4

Consider the Hermitian random matrix of size
N
,
B=N\cdot A+N\theta E_{1,1}
where
A
is a GUE matrix. Relation
(
96
) implies that the
1/N
correction of
A+\theta E_{1,1}
is given by

\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}\left(x^{k-m-1}\frac{\theta}{1-\theta x}\right)\right|_{x=0}

(97)

=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\binom{k}{m+1}\oint_{|z|=1}\frac{\theta z^{k}}{1-\theta z}\left(\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right)^{m+1}d\text{}z=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}\frac{\theta z^{k}}{1-\theta z}\sum_{m=1}^{k}\binom{k}{m}\left(\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right)^{m}d\text{}z

=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}\frac{\theta z^{k}}{1-\theta z}\left(\left(1+\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right)^{k}-1\right)d\text{}z=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}\frac{\theta}{1-\theta z}\left(z+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k}d\text{}z,

for
|\theta|<1
. Making the change of variables
u=2\cos\text{}t

on the last contour integral, we deduce that

\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}\left(x^{k-m-1}\frac{\theta}{1-\theta x}\right)\right|_{x=0}=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}\frac{\theta(u-2\theta)}{(\theta(u-\theta)-1)\sqrt{4-u^{2}}}u^{k}d\text{}u,

for every
k\geq 1
. The above equality gives a characterization of the
1/N
correction of
A+\theta E_{1,1}
via a signed measure for the case

|\theta|<1
, in the sense that (
97
) is given by the
k
-th
moment of

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{\theta}(d\text{}u)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\boldsymbol{1}_{(-2,2)}(u)\frac{\theta(u-2\theta)}{(\theta(u-\theta)-1)\sqrt{4-u^{2}}}d\text{}u,

which has total mass zero. In order to get an integral representation that
will lead to the signed measure, we write (
97
) in the form

\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{1}{m!}\binom{k}{m+1}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta x}\left(x^{k-m-1}-\left(\frac{1}{\theta}\right)^{k-m-1}\right)\right)\right|_{x=0}+\left(\theta+\frac{1}{\theta}\right)^{k}-\frac{1}{\theta^{k}},

where by Cauchy formula the sum is equal to

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\binom{k}{m+1}\oint_{|z|=1}\frac{\theta}{z^{m+1}(1-\theta z)}\left(z^{k-m-1}-\frac{1}{\theta^{k-m-1}}\right)d\text{}z

=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}\left(\frac{\theta z^{k}}{1-\theta z}\sum_{m=1}^{k}\binom{k}{m}\frac{1}{z^{2m}}-\frac{\theta}{\theta^{k}(1-\theta z)}\sum_{m=1}^{k}\binom{k}{m}\frac{\theta^{m+1}}{z^{m+1}}\right)d\text{}z

=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta z}\left(z+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k}-\frac{\theta z^{k}}{1-\theta z}-\frac{\theta}{1-\theta z}\left(\frac{1}{\theta}+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k}+\frac{\theta}{\theta^{k}(1-\theta z)}\right)d\text{}z

=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}\frac{\theta}{1-\theta z}\left(\left(z+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k}-\left(\frac{1}{\theta}+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k}\right)d\text{}z.

In the last contour integral it is visible that the
k
-th moment of the
signed measure will emerge from the function
\frac{\theta}{1-\theta z}\left(z+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k}
. Thus, using that

\oint_{|z|=1}\frac{\theta}{1-\theta z}\left(\left(\frac{1}{z}+\frac{1}{\theta}\right)^{k}-\left(\theta+\frac{1}{\theta}\right)^{k}\right)d\text{}z=\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}\frac{\theta}{z}\left(\frac{1}{z}+\frac{1}{\theta}\right)^{n}\left(\theta+\frac{1}{\theta}\right)^{k-1-n}d\text{}z

=\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}\frac{1}{\theta^{n-1}}\left(\theta+\frac{1}{\theta}\right)^{k-1-n}=\left(\theta+\frac{1}{\theta}\right)^{k}-\frac{1}{\theta^{k}},

we obtain, for
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, that (
97
) is equal to

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}\frac{\theta}{1-\theta z}\left(\left(z+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k}-\left(\theta+\frac{1}{\theta}\right)^{k}\right)d\text{}z=\boldsymbol{1}_{|\theta|\geq 1}\left(\theta+\frac{1}{\theta}\right)^{k}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}\frac{\theta(u-2\theta)u^{k}}{(\theta(u-\theta)-1)\sqrt{4-u^{2}}}d\text{}u.

By the above, we deduce that the
1/N
correction of the matrix
A+\sum_{i=1}^{l}\theta_{i}E_{i,i}
, in the sense of (
89
), is given
by a linear functional
\varphi^{\prime}:\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, where for every polynomial
P
,

\varphi^{\prime}(P)=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\int_{\mathbb{R}}P(t)\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{\theta_{i}}(d\text{}t),\text{}

and for every
i=1,\ldots,l
,
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{\theta_{i}}
are
signed measures on
\mathbb{R}
of total mass zero given by

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{\theta_{i}}(d\text{}t)=\boldsymbol{1}_{|\theta_{i}|\geq 1}\delta\left(\theta_{i}+\theta_{i}^{-1}\right)(d\text{}t)-\frac{1}{2\pi}\boldsymbol{1}_{(-2,2)}(t)\frac{\theta_{i}(t-2\theta_{i})}{(\theta_{i}(t-\theta_{i})-1)\sqrt{4-t^{2}}}d\text{}t.

This repeats a result of
[
Sh
]
. The delta measures involved in the correction measure illustrate the Baik-Ben Arous-Peche phase transition, first studied in
[
BBP
]
.

Example 5

Consider the Hermitian random matrix of size
N
defined by
A=N\cdot A_{1}+N\theta E_{1,1}
, where
A_{1}
is a Wishart matrix as in Example
3
. Relation
(
96
) implies that the
k
-th moment of the
1/N
correction measure of
A_{1}+\theta E_{1,1}
is given by

\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}\left(\left(\frac{\lambda}{1-x}\right)^{k-m-1}\frac{\theta}{1-\theta x}\right)\right|_{x=0}.

(98)

In order to use an integral representation for the derivative that will
simplify the computation of the signed measure, we write

\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}\left(\left(\frac{1}{1-x}\right)^{k-m-1}\frac{\theta}{1-\theta x}\right)\right|_{x=0}=\sum_{n=0}^{m}\frac{m!}{n!}\theta^{m-n+1}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}}{\mathrm{d}x^{n}}(1+x)^{k-m+n-2}\right|_{x=0}

=\sum_{n=0}^{m}\frac{m!\theta^{m-n+1}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}}\frac{(1+z)^{k-m+n-2}}{z^{n+1}}d\text{}z=\frac{m!\theta}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}}\frac{(1+z)^{k-m-2}}{z}\sum_{n=0}^{m}\left(1+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{n}\theta^{m-n}d\text{}z

=\frac{m!\theta}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}}\frac{(1+z)^{k-m-2}}{(1-\theta)z+1}\left(\left(1+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{m+1}-\theta^{m+1}\right)d\text{}z

=\frac{m!\theta}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}}\frac{(1+z)^{k-1}}{(1-\theta)z+1}\left(\frac{1}{z^{m+1}}-\left(\frac{\theta}{1+z}\right)^{m+1}\right)d\text{}z,

for
k\geq 2
. Thus, (
98
) is equal to

\frac{\theta\lambda}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}}\frac{(\lambda+\lambda z)^{k-1}}{(1-\theta)z+1}\sum_{m=0}^{k-2}\binom{k}{m+1}\left(\left(\frac{1}{\lambda z}\right)^{m+1}-\left(\frac{\theta}{\lambda(1+z)}\right)^{m+1}\right)d\text{}z+\theta^{k}

=\frac{\theta}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}}\frac{(1+z)^{-1}}{(1-\theta)z+1}\left(\left(\lambda+1+\lambda z+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k}-\left(1+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k}-(\lambda+\theta+\lambda z)^{k}+\theta^{k}\right)d\text{}z+\theta^{k}.

Similarly with Example
3
, in the above contour integration the function

\frac{(1+z)^{-1}}{(1-\theta)z+1}\left(\lambda+1+\lambda z+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k}

will give the density of a signed measure while the remaining terms will
contribute to point masses. We will only treat the case
\lambda=1

because it illustrates the procedure sufficiently. By Cauchy formula, a
straightforward computation shows that

\frac{\theta}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}\frac{(1+z)^{-1}}{(1-\theta)z+1}\left(1+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k}d\text{}z=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\theta^{k}\text{, for }|\theta-1|<1\text{}\\
0\text{, for }|\theta-1|>1\end{array}\right.

and

\frac{\theta}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}\frac{(1+z)^{-1}}{(1-\theta)z+1}((\theta+1+z)^{k}-\theta^{k})d\text{}z=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}0\text{, for }|\theta-1|<1\\
\left(\theta+1+\frac{1}{\theta-1}\right)^{k}-\theta^{k}\text{,
for }|\theta-1|>1.\end{array}\right.

As a corollary, the corresponding linear functional
\varphi^{\prime}_{\theta}

that gives the
1/N
correction satisfies the relation

\varphi^{\prime}_{\theta}(\mathbf{x}^{k})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{4}\frac{\theta(2-\theta)}{(1-\theta)t+\theta^{2}}\frac{t^{k}}{\sqrt{(4-t)t}}d\text{}t+\boldsymbol{1}_{|\theta-1|>1}\left(\theta+1+\frac{1}{\theta-1}\right)^{k},

for every
k\geq 1
and
|\theta-1|\neq 1
.

6
Infinitesimal quantized freeness

In this section we deal with a different setup compared to the rest of the paper.
However, this setup is a closely related one and leads to new classes of applications.
Instead of the study of the Harish-Chandra transform, we will study characters of the
irreducible representations of the unitary group
U(N)
, as
N\rightarrow\infty
.

We start by recalling relevant definitions and the main result of
[
BG
]
. It is well-known that all irreducible representations of
U(N)
are parametrized by signatures(=highest weights), that is,
N
-tuples of integers

\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{N}
. We denote
by
\hat{U}(N)
the set of all signatures and by
\pi^{\lambda}
the
irreducible representation of
U(N)
that corresponds to
\lambda\in\hat{U}(N)
.
Information about a signature
\lambda
can be encoded by a discrete
probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
:

\boldsymbol{m}_{N}[\lambda]:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta\left(\frac{\lambda_{i}+N-i}{N}\right).

(99)

For
\lambda\in\hat{U}(N)
chosen at random with respect to some
probability measure
\varrho
on
\hat{U}(N)
, we are interested in the
asymptotic behaviour of the random measure (
99
).

We recall that for a finite-dimensional representation
\pi
of
U(N)
, the
character of
\pi
is the function given by
\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\pi}(U):=\operatorname{Tr}(\pi(U))
, for every
U\in U(N)
. Note that for a matrix
U\in U(N)

with eigenvalues
u_{1},\ldots,u_{N}
, one has
\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\pi}(U)=\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\pi}(\operatorname{diag}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N}))
, and the character will be denoted
by
\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\pi}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N})
. Moreover, for
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{N})\in\hat{U}(N)
we will denote
the character of
\pi^{\lambda}
by
\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\lambda}
. Due
to Weyl (see, e.g.,
[
W
]
), for
U\in U(N)
with eigenvalues
u_{1},\ldots,u_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
, the value
\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\lambda}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N})

is given by the rational Schur function, i.e., we have

\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\lambda}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N})=\frac{\det(u_{i}^{\lambda_{j}+N-j})}{\det(u_{i}^{N-j})}=\frac{\det(u_{i}^{\lambda_{j}+N-j})}{\prod_{i<j}(u_{i}-u_{j})}.

(100)

Note that
\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\lambda}(1^{N})
is the dimension of

\pi^{\lambda}
.

Let
\varrho
be a probability measure on
\hat{U}(N)
. For a fixed
\lambda\in\hat{U}(N)
, the symmetric polynomial

\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\lambda}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N})/\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\lambda}(1^{N})

should be thought of as
the analogue of the Harish-Chandra integral
H\text{}C(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N};\lambda_{1}(A),\ldots,\lambda_{N}(A))
of a Hermitian random matrix of size

N
with fixed spectrum
\{\lambda_{i}(A)\}_{i=1}^{N}
. An analog of the Harish-Chandra transform is the following notion.

Definition 5

For a probability measure
\varrho
on
\hat{U}(N)
, a
Schur generating function

S_{\varrho}^{U(N)}
is a symmetric Laurent
power series in
(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N})
given by

S_{\varrho}^{U(N)}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N}):=\sum_{\lambda\in\hat{U}(N)}\varrho(\lambda)\frac{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\lambda}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N})}{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\lambda}(1^{N})}.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
we consider the
following differential operator acting on smooth functions
f
of
N

variables:

\mathcal{D}_{k}^{U(N)}(f):=\left(\prod_{i<j}(u_{i}-u_{j})\right)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(u_{i}\partial_{i})^{k}\left(\prod_{i<j}(u_{i}-u_{j})f\right).

Proposition 2

For every
\lambda\in\hat{U}(N)
, the character

\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\lambda}
satisfies the relation

\mathcal{D}_{k}^{U(N)}(\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\lambda})(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\lambda_{i}+N-i)^{k}\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\lambda}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N}).

Proof.

See, e.g.,
[
BG
]
.
∎

The operator
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{U(N)}
is quite similar to the operator

\mathcal{D}_{k}
used in previous sections.

Theorem 13

(Bufetov-Gorin, Theorem 5.1)

Let
\varrho(N)
be a sequence of probability measures on

\hat{U}(N)
such that for every
r\in\mathbb{N}
one has

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{r},1^{N-r})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\Psi(u_{i}),

(101)

where
\Psi
is an analytic function in a complex neighborhood of
1
and the above
convergence is uniform in a complex neighborhood of
1^{r}
. Then the random measure

N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta\left(\frac{\lambda_{i}+N-i}{N}\right)
, converges,
as
N\rightarrow\infty
, in probability, in the sense of moments to a
deterministic measure
\mathbf{m}
on
\mathbb{R}
, whose moments are given
by

\int_{\mathbb{R}}t^{k}\mathbf{m}(d\text{}t)=\sum_{m=0}^{k}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}(x^{k}(\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m})\right|_{x=1}.

(102)

In the setting of
irreducible representations of
U(N)
and Theorem
13
, the tensor product of representations can be viewed as a discrete generalization of a summation of (random) matrices. Let
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
be two finite-dimensional representations of
U(N)
, their tensor product
(\pi_{1}\otimes\pi_{2})(U)
,
for
U\in U(N)
, is the Kronecker product of the matrices
\pi_{1}(U),\pi_{2}(U)
. This implies that
\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\pi_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}}(U)=\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\pi_{1}}(U)\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\pi_{2}}(U)
. Assume that
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
are irreducible representations of
U(N)
that correspond to
signatures
\lambda_{1}(N),\lambda_{2}(N)
, respectively. Moreover,
assuming that for every fixed
r
and
i=1,2
, one has

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\lambda_{i}(N)}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{r},1^{N-r})}{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\lambda_{i}(N)}(1^{N})}\right)=\Psi_{i}(u_{1})+\cdots+\Psi_{i}(u_{r}),

(103)

uniformly in a neighborhood of
1^{r}
, we can include the asymptotics of

\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\pi_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}}
to the context of Theorem

13
. Assumption (
103
) should be thought of as the analogue
of (
40
) for the matrices
A_{1},A_{2}
.

The representation
\pi_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}
can be decomposed into irreducibles

\pi_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\hat{U}(N)}c_{\lambda}\pi^{\lambda},

where the non-negative integers
c_{\lambda}
are multiplicities. This
implies that the character of
\pi=\pi_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}
is equal to

S_{\varrho^{\pi}}^{U(N)}
, where the probability measure
\varrho^{\pi}
on

\hat{U}(N)
is given by

\varrho^{\pi}(\lambda)=\frac{c_{\lambda}\dim(\pi^{\lambda})}{\dim(\pi_{1}\otimes\pi_{2})}\text{, \quad for every }\lambda\in\hat{U}(N).

Hence, we have the following result.

Corollary 1

(Bufetov-Gorin)

Let
\lambda_{1}(N),\lambda_{2}(N)\in\hat{U}(N)

be two sequences of signatures such that for
i=1,2
,

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\boldsymbol{m}_{N}[\lambda_{i}(N)]=\mathbf{m}_{i},

where
\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{m}_{2}
are two probability measures and the
above convergence is weak. Moreover, let
\pi(N)=\pi^{\lambda_{1}(N)}\otimes\pi^{\lambda_{2}(N)}
. Then, for
\lambda(N)\in\hat{U}(N)
chosen at random with respect to
\varrho^{\pi(N)}
, the random
measures
\boldsymbol{m}_{N}[\lambda(N)]
converge, as
N\rightarrow\infty
, in the sense of moments, in probability to a deterministic
probability measure which one denotes by
\mathbf{m}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{m}_{2}
.

This corollary is an analogue of Voiculescu’s theorem for random
matrices, see Theorem
6
above. The free
convolution
\mathbf{m}_{1}\boxplus\mathbf{m}_{2}
from Theorem
6
is replaced by
\mathbf{m}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{m}_{2}
in Corollary
1
. In contrast with the random matrix framework, it was
noticed in
[
BG
]
that the operation
(\mathbf{m}_{1},\mathbf{m}_{2})\mapsto\mathbf{m}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{m}_{2}
is not linearized by the
R
-transform.
Instead, it is linearized by its deformation

R_{\mathbf{m}}^{q\text{}u\text{}a\text{}n\text{}t}(z):=R_{\mathbf{m}}(z)-R_{u[0,1]}(z),

where
u[0,1]
stands for the uniform measure on
[0,1]
. This means that

R^{q\text{}u\text{}a\text{}n\text{}t}_{\mathbf{m}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{m}_{2}}(z)=R^{q\text{}u\text{}a\text{}n\text{}t}_{\mathbf{m}_{1}}(z)+R_{\mathbf{m}_{2}}^{q\text{}u\text{}a\text{}n\text{}t}(z)
.
The map
R_{\mathbf{m}}^{q\text{}u\text{}a\text{}n\text{}t}
was
introduced in
[
BG
]
and it is called the quantized
R
-transform, while the operation
\mathbf{m}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{m}_{2}
is called the quantized free convolution
of
\mathbf{m}_{1}
and
\mathbf{m}_{2}
.

In the following we concentrate on the
1/N
correction of the random
measure (
99
), as
N\rightarrow\infty
. First we focus on its
description via an explicit formula (as it was done in Theorem

12
for the empirical distribution of random matrices).

Theorem 14

Let
\varrho(N)
be a sequence of probability measures on

\hat{U}(N)
such that for every finite
r
one has

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N\left(\frac{1}{N}\log S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{r},1^{N-r})-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\Psi(u_{i})\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\Phi(u_{i}),

(104)

where
\Psi,\Phi
are analytic functions in a neighborhood of
1
and the
above convergence is uniform in a neighborhood of
1^{r}
. Then, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\lambda\in\hat{U}(N)}\varrho(N)[\lambda]\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{\lambda_{i}+N-i}{N}\right)^{k}-\mathbf{m}_{k}\right)=\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{k},

(105)

where
(\mathbf{m}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are given by (
102
) and

\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{k}=\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}\left(x^{k}\left(\Phi^{\prime}(x)-\frac{1}{2x}\right)(\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-1}\right)\right|_{x=1}.

(106)

Proof.

The proof strategy is similar to the proof of Theorem
12
. Assumption
(
104
) implies that (
101
) holds. This
allows us to write

\sum_{\lambda\in\hat{U}(N)}\varrho(N)[\lambda]\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\lambda_{i}+N-i)^{k}

(107)

in the form
N^{k+1}m_{k,N}(\Psi)+N^{k}m_{k,N}(\Psi,\Phi)+\omicron(N^{k})
, where
(m_{k,N}(\Psi))_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to (
102
)
and we need to prove that
(m_{k,N}(\Psi,\Phi))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
(
106
). By Proposition
2
,
(
107
) is equal to
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{U(N)}S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}{\mbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-90.0}{${-\!\!-}$}}}_{u_{i}=1}
. Due to the definition of the differential
operator,
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{U(N)}S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}
can be written as
a linear combination of terms

\left(\prod_{i<j}(u_{i}-u_{j})\right)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}u_{i}^{n}\partial_{i}^{n}\left(\prod_{i<j}(u_{i}-u_{j})S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right),

(108)

for
n=1,\ldots,k
. However, only for
n=k-1
and
n=k
the terms
(
108
) contribute to the left-hand side of
(
105
) and their coefficients are
\frac{k(k-1)}{2}

and
1
, respectively. For
n=k
, by writing
S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}=\exp\left(N\cdot\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)
and
using the chain rule in order to compute the derivatives of
S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}
, we obtain the expansion of (
108
) as a
linear combination of terms of the form

\frac{u_{b_{0}}^{k}\left(\partial_{b_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)\right)^{l_{1}}\ldots\left(\partial_{b_{0}}^{k-m}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)\right)^{l_{k-m}}}{(u_{b_{0}}-u_{b_{1}})\ldots(u_{b_{0}}-u_{b_{m}})}+\text{{\hskip 140.00021pt}}

\text{{\hskip 100.00015pt}}\ldots+\frac{u_{b_{m}}^{k}\left(\partial_{b_{m}}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)\right)^{l_{1}}\ldots\left(\partial_{b_{m}}^{k-m}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)\right)^{l_{k-m}}}{(u_{b_{m}}-u_{b_{0}})\ldots(u_{b_{m}}-u_{b_{m-1}})},

(109)

where
m\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
,
b_{0},\ldots,b_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and

l_{1},\ldots,l_{k-m}
are non-negative integers such that
l_{1}+2l_{2}+\cdots+(k-m)l_{k-m}=k-m
. Similarly with the proof of Theorem

8
, setting
u_{i}=1+i\varepsilon
for every
i=1,\ldots,N
, and considering the
m
-th order Taylor polynomial of

F_{i}(\varepsilon)=\left.u_{i}^{k}\left(\partial_{i}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)\right)^{l_{1}}\ldots\left(\partial_{i}^{k-m}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)\right)^{l_{k-m}}\right|_{u_{j}=1+j\varepsilon},

for
i=b_{0},\ldots,b_{m}
, we obtain that, as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
,
(
109
) is a linear combination of derivatives

\partial_{i_{m}}\ldots\partial_{i_{1}}\left[u_{i}^{k}\left(\partial_{i}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)\right)^{l_{1}}\ldots\left(\partial_{i}^{k-m}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)\right)^{l_{k-m}}\right](1^{N}).

The coefficients of these derivatives do not depend on
N
because
(
109
) emerges from differentiating
k-m
times the function

S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}
in (
108
), for
n=k
.
Since (
109
) does not depend on
b_{0},\ldots,b_{m}
when
u_{i}=1+i\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, we deduce that

m_{k,N}(\Psi)=\sum_{m=0}^{k}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m)!}\partial_{1}^{m}\left(u_{1}^{k}\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)\right)^{k-m}\right)(1^{N})+\omicron(N^{-1}).

Assumption (
104
) allows us to control
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N(m_{k,N}(\Psi)-\mathbf{m}_{k})
. Namely, we start with the equality

N\left(\partial_{1}^{m}\left(u_{1}^{k}\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)\right)^{k-m}\right)(1^{N})-\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}u^{m}}(u^{k}(\Psi^{\prime}(u))^{k-m})\right|_{u=1}\right)

=\sum_{l_{0}+\cdots+l_{k-m}=m}\frac{m!}{l_{0}!\ldots l_{k-m}!}\frac{k!}{(k-l_{0})!}N\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k-m}\partial_{1}^{l_{i}+1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)(1^{N})-\prod_{i=1}^{k-m}\Psi^{(l_{i}+1)}(1)\right),

and due to (
104
) we have

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k-m}\partial_{1}^{l_{i}+1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)(1^{N})-\prod_{i=1}^{k-m}\Psi^{(l_{i}+1)}(1)\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{k-m}\Phi^{(l_{i}+1)}(1)\prod_{\underset{j\neq i}{j=1}}^{k-m}\Psi^{(l_{j}+1)}(1).

Therefore,

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N(m_{k,N}(\Psi)-\mathbf{m}_{k})=\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}(x^{k}\Phi^{\prime}(x)(\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-1})\right|_{x=1}.

(110)

It remains to show that

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}m_{k,N}(\Psi,\Phi)=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}(x^{k-1}(\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-1})\right|_{x=1}.

(111)

Both for
n=k-1
and
n=k
, the terms (
108
)
contribute to
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}m_{k,N}(\Psi,\Phi)
. As we
showed above, for
n=k-1
the contribution is

\frac{k(k-1)}{2}\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{(k-1)!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}(x^{k-1}(\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-1})\right|_{x=1}.

(112)

The case
n=k
is quite similar to what we did in Theorem

12
. When
u_{1}=\cdots=u_{N}=1
, the sum with respect to

b_{0},\ldots,b_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
of (
109
)
(multiplied by its coefficient, which is
O(N^{l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}})
)
gives a term that contributes to
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}m_{k,N}(\Psi,\Phi)
when
l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}\in\{k-m-1,k-m\}
. For

l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}=k-m
, the contribution is

-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m+1}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m+1}}(x^{k}(\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-1})\right|_{x=1}.

(113)

On the other hand, for
l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}=k-m-1
, the
contribution is

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m=0}^{k-2}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-2)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}(x^{k}\Psi^{\prime\prime}(x)(\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-2})\right|_{x=1}.

(114)

Summing the expressions from (
112
),
(
113
) and (
114
), we see that (
111
) holds.
This proves the claim.
∎

Our next goal is to give a description of the above
1/N
correction in terms of the
infinitesimal quantized free probability. Namely, we would like to
understand how to express moments (
102
) via the moment-cumulant formula (
29
). This would allow to introduce the infinitesimal quantized free cumulants, which should be a non-trivial deformation of infinitesimal free cumulants.

Interestingly enough, we were unable to prove an analog of Lemma
5
in the discrete setup directly. Instead, below we are essentially proving Theorem
14
again in a slightly different way, which will produce a required formula.
The key point is to deform the Schur generating
function.

Definition 6

A deformed Schur generating function is a symmetric function

T_{\varrho}^{U(N)}
given by

T_{\varrho}^{U(N)}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N})=\frac{\prod_{i<j}(e^{u_{i}}-e^{u_{j}})}{\prod_{i<j}(u_{i}-u_{j})}S_{\varrho}^{U(N)}(e^{u_{1}},\ldots,e^{u_{N}}).

For our purpose, the usefulness of
T_{\varrho}^{U(N)}
comes from the fact
that the function

(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N})\longmapsto\frac{\det(e^{u_{i}(\lambda_{j}+N-j)})_{1\leq i,j\leq N}}{\prod_{i<j}(u_{i}-u_{j})\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\lambda}(1^{N})}

is an eigenfunction of
\mathcal{D}_{k}
with corresponding eigenvalue
\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\lambda_{i}+N-i)^{k}
. Moreover, an additive asymptotic behaviour for
the logarithm of
S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}
implies the same phenomenon for the
logarithm of
T_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}
.

Theorem 15

Let
\varrho(N)
be a sequence of probability measures on
\hat{U}(N)

such that for every finite
r
the condition (
104
)
is satisfied. Then, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\lambda\in\hat{U}(N)}\varrho(N)[\lambda]\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{\lambda_{i}+N-i}{N}\right)^{k}-\mathbf{m}_{k}\right)=\sum_{\pi\in\operatorname{NC}(k)}\sum_{V\in\pi}\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{|V|}^{(1)}\prod_{\underset{W\neq V}{W\in\pi}}\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{|W|}^{(0)},

(115)

where
(\mathbf{m}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are given by (
102
) and
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{n}^{(0)}:=\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}}{\mathrm{d}u^{n}}\left(\Psi(e^{u})+\log\left(\frac{e^{u}-1}{u}\right)\right)\right|_{u=0}\text{\hskip 10.22217ptand\hskip 10.22217pt}\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{n}^{(1)}:=\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}}{\mathrm{d}u^{n}}\left(\Phi(e^{u})-\frac{1}{2}u\right)\right|_{u=0}.

Proof.

Note that condition (
104
) determines the
asymptotics of the logarithm of
T_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}
, since for every
finite
r
we have

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}T_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{r},0^{N-r})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\Psi(e^{u_{i}})+\log\left(\frac{e^{u_{i}}-1}{u_{i}}\right),

(116)

where the above convergence is uniform in a complex neighborhood of
0^{r}
. Thus, we
have

\mathbf{m}_{k}=\sum_{m=0}^{k}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}u^{m}}((A^{\prime}(u)+B^{\prime}(u))^{k-m})\right|_{u=0},

(117)

where
A(u):=\Psi(e^{u})
and
B(u):=\log(e^{u}-1)-\log\text{}(u)
. This holds because both the left and the right hand side of
(
117
) are equal to
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{-k-1}\mathcal{D}_{k}T_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}{\mbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-90.0}{${-\!\!-}$}}}_{u_{i}=0}
. Moreover, as
we showed, (
116
) implies that for
N^{-k-1}\mathcal{D}_{k}T_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}{\mbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-90.0}{${-\!\!-}$}}}_{u_{i}=0}
we can get an expansion of
the form
M_{0,k,N}+N^{-1}M_{1,k,N}+\ldots
, where the sequences

(M_{i,k,N})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converge. In the proof of Theorem

9
we justified why only the sequence
(M_{0,k,N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
will contribute to the limit (
115
). We recall that

M_{0,k,N}
is a linear combination of derivatives

\partial_{i_{m}}\ldots\partial_{i_{1}}\left(\partial_{i_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}T_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)\right)^{k-m}(0^{N}),

(118)

for
m=0,\ldots,k-1
, where the coefficients do not depend on
N
. The
summand
M_{0,k,N}^{(1)}
of
M_{0,k,N}
, that involves derivatives
(
118
) where
i_{0}=\cdots=i_{m}
, is

M_{0,k,N}^{(1)}=\sum_{m=0}^{k}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m!)}\partial_{1}^{m}\left(\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}{T^{U(N)}_{\varrho(N)}}\right)\right)^{k-m}\right)(0^{N}).

Due to (
104
), for every non-negative integer

l
, one has

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N\left(\partial_{1}^{l}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}T_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)(0^{N})-A^{(l)}(0)-B^{(l)}(0)\right)=\Gamma^{(l)}(0)-B^{(l)}(0),

where
\Gamma(u):=\Phi(e^{u})
. We deduce that

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N(M_{0,k,N}^{(1)}-\mathbf{m}_{k})=\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}u^{m}}((\Gamma^{\prime}(u)-B^{\prime}(u))(A^{\prime}(u)+B^{\prime}(u))^{k-m-1})\right|_{u=0}.

Moreover, since

N\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}T_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{r},0^{N-r})-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\Psi(e^{u_{i}})+\log\left(\frac{e^{u_{i}}-1}{u_{i}}\right)\right)=\log\left(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq r}\frac{e^{u_{i}}-e^{u_{j}}}{u_{i}-u_{j}}\right)

-r\text{}\log\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}\frac{e^{u_{i}}-1}{u_{i}}\right)+N\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}S_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}(e^{u_{1}},\ldots,e^{u_{r}},1^{N-r})-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\Psi(e^{u_{i}})\right),

condition (
104
) implies that only the summands

M_{0,k,N}^{(1)},M_{0,k,N}^{(2)}
of
M_{0,k,N}
will contribute to
the limit (
115
), where
M_{0,k,N}^{(2)}
is the summand that
involves all the derivatives (
118
), where
|\{i_{0},\ldots,i_{m}\}|=2
. From the procedure that we described in the proof of Theorem

8
that provides the expansion for
N^{-k-1}\mathcal{D}_{k}T_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}{\mbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-90.0}{${-\!\!-}$}}}_{u_{i}=0}
, we obtain that

N\cdot\text{}M_{0,k,N}^{(2)}=-\sum_{m=1}^{k-1}\frac{k!N}{m!(m+1)!(k-m)!}\sum_{n=1}^{m}\binom{m}{n}\partial_{2}^{n}\partial_{1}^{m-n}\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}T_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)\right)^{k-m}(0^{N}).

In order to compute its limit as
N\rightarrow\infty
, we write

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N\partial_{2}^{n}\partial_{1}^{m-n}\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}T_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)\right)^{k-m}(0^{N})=

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}=m-n}\frac{(m-n)!N}{l_{1}!\ldots l_{k-m}!}\sum_{i=1}^{k-m}\partial_{2}^{n}\partial_{1}^{l_{i}+1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}T_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)(0^{N})\prod_{\underset{j\neq i}{j=1}}^{k-m}\partial_{1}^{l_{j}+1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}T_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)(0^{N})

=(-1)^{n}(k-m)\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m-n}}{\mathrm{d}u^{m-n}}(B^{(n+1)}(u)(A^{\prime}(u)+B^{\prime}(u))^{k-m-1})\right|_{u=0},

where we used that
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\partial_{2}^{n}\partial_{1}^{l}\left(\log\text{}T_{\varrho(N)}^{U(N)}\right)(0^{N})=(-1)^{n}B^{(l+n)}(0)
. As a corollary,
(N\cdot M_{0,k,N}^{(2)})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

will converge to

\sum_{m=1}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\sum_{n=1}^{m}(-1)^{n+1}\binom{m}{n}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m-n}}{\mathrm{d}u^{m-n}}(B^{(n+1)}(u)(A^{\prime}(u)+B^{\prime}(u))^{k-m-1})\right|_{u=0}

=\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}u^{m}}\left(\left(B^{\prime}(u)-\frac{1}{2}\right)(A^{\prime}(u)+B^{\prime}(u))^{k-m-1}\right)\right|_{u=0},

because
B^{\prime}(0)=1/2
. Thus, due to Lemma
5
,
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N(M_{0,k,N}-\mathbf{m}_{k})
is equal to the right hand side of
(
115
), which proves the claim.
∎

Remark 4

Theorem
15
describes the ”differentiation procedure” of
the moment-cumulant relations of the limit measure from Theorem

13
that gives the
1/N
correction. In particular, for the
limit regime (
104
) we showed that the ”derivative” of

u[0,1]
is
\delta\left(-1/2\right)
.

In the remainder of the section, we provide some examples. Our first
example comes from
extreme characters
of
U(\infty)
. Their
multiplicativity incorporates them into the framework of Theorem
13

and Theorem
14
. In that sense, they are analogous to ergodic
unitarily invariant matrices on
H(\infty)
.

We recall that a character
\boldsymbol{\chi}:U(\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
has a form

\boldsymbol{\chi}(\operatorname{diag}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N},1,1,\ldots))=\sum_{\lambda\in\hat{U}(N)}\varrho^{\boldsymbol{\chi}}(N)[\lambda]\frac{s_{\lambda}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N})}{s_{\lambda}(1^{N})},

where
s_{\lambda}
is the rational Schur function, given by
(
100
), and
\varrho^{\boldsymbol{\chi}}(N)
is a probability measure on

\hat{U}(N)
. By extreme characters we refer to the extreme points of the
convex set of characters of
U(\infty)
. The classification of the extreme
characters is known as the Edrei-Voiculescu theorem.
They are multiplicative functions, meaning that

\boldsymbol{\chi}(U)=\prod_{u\in\operatorname{Spec}(U)}F(u),

where
F
is a function of one variable, parametrized by a set of non-negative
parameters. It is known as Voiculescu function and its general form can be
found in
[
E
]
,
[
V3
]
, or, e.g.,
[
BBO
]
. For our particular example, we consider

F(u)=\exp(\delta(u-1))\frac{1}{1-\alpha(u-1)},

(119)

where
\delta,\alpha\geq 0
.

Example 6

Let
\chi^{\pi(N)}
be the character of
U(N)
that is the restriction on
U(N)
of the character

\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\pi}
given by the function (
119
), where

\delta=N\gamma
and
\alpha,\gamma\geq 0
do not depend on
N
. Its decomposition into irreducible characters define a probability measure on
\hat{U}(N)
.
Its Schur generating function
\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\pi(N)}(\operatorname{diag}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N}))
will satisfy
(
104
) for
\Psi(u)=\gamma(u-1)
and
\Phi(u)=-\log(1-\alpha(u-1))
. Therefore, the limiting probability measure that
emerges form Theorem
13
has
k
-th moment

\sum_{m=0}^{k}\frac{k!\gamma^{k-m}}{m!(m+1)!(k-m)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}u^{m}}(u+1)^{k}\right|_{u=0}.

By Cauchy formula, this expression equals

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}(k+1)}\oint_{|z|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma}}}\frac{1}{z+1}\left(\gamma z+\frac{1}{z}+\gamma+1\right)^{k+1}d\text{}z

=\frac{1}{2\pi(k+1)}\int_{\gamma+1-2\sqrt{\gamma}}^{\gamma+1+2\sqrt{\gamma}}\frac{t+1-\gamma}{t}\frac{t^{k+1}}{\sqrt{(\gamma+1+2\sqrt{\gamma}-t)(t-\gamma-1+2\sqrt{\gamma})}}d\text{}t.

Integrating by parts, we obtain that the density function of
the limit probability measure is

\frac{1}{\pi}\boldsymbol{1}_{[\gamma+1-2\sqrt{\gamma},\gamma+1+2\sqrt{\gamma}]}(x)\arccos\left(\frac{x-1+\gamma}{2\sqrt{\gamma x}}\right),

for
\gamma\geq 1
and

\frac{1}{\pi}\boldsymbol{1}_{[\gamma+1-2\sqrt{\gamma},\gamma+1+2\sqrt{\gamma}]}(x)\arccos\left(\frac{x-1+\gamma}{2\sqrt{\gamma x}}\right)+\boldsymbol{1}_{[0,\gamma+1-2\sqrt{\gamma}]}(x),

for
0<\gamma<1
.
To simplify the formulas, let us assume that
\gamma>1
.
For the correction measure, by Theorem
14
its
k
-th moment is given by

\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}u^{m}}\left(u^{k}\frac{\alpha\gamma^{k-m-1}}{1-\alpha(u-1)}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}u^{m}}(u^{k-1}\gamma^{k-m-1})\right)\right|_{u=1}.

(120)

After opening the parenthesis, the first summand can be written in the form

\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}u^{m}}\left((u+1)^{k}\frac{\alpha\gamma^{k-m-1}}{1-\alpha u}\right)\right|_{u=0}=\left(\alpha+\gamma+1+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\right)^{k}-\left(\gamma+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\right)^{k}+

\text{{\hskip 80.00012pt}}+\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}u^{m}}\left(\left((u+1)^{k}-\left(1+\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)^{k}\right)\frac{\alpha\gamma^{k-m-1}}{1-\alpha u}\right)\right|_{u=0}.

Using the Cauchy formula and doing similar computations as in Example

4
, we obtain that the above sum is equal to

\frac{\alpha}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma}}}\frac{1}{1-\alpha z}\left(\left(\gamma z+\gamma+1+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k}-(\gamma z+\gamma)^{k}-\left(\frac{1}{z}+\frac{1}{\alpha z}+\gamma+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\right)^{k}+\left(\gamma+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\right)^{k}\right)d\text{}z\text{}

=\frac{\alpha}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma}}}\frac{1}{1-\alpha z}\left(\left(\gamma z+\gamma+1+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k}-\left(\alpha+1+\gamma+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\right)^{k}\right)d\text{}z\text{}-\left(\alpha+1+\gamma+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\right)^{k}+\left(\gamma+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\right)^{k}.

For
\sqrt{\gamma}<\alpha
, the above contour integral is equal to

\frac{\alpha}{2\pi}\int_{\gamma+1-2\sqrt{\gamma}}^{\gamma+1+2\sqrt{\gamma}}\frac{t-2\alpha-\gamma-1}{\gamma+\alpha^{2}+\alpha\gamma+\alpha-\alpha t}\frac{t^{k}}{\sqrt{(\gamma+1+2\sqrt{\gamma}-t)(t-\gamma-1+2\sqrt{\gamma})}}d\text{}t+\left(\alpha+1+\gamma+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\right)^{k}.

(121)

In the opposite case
\alpha<\sqrt{\gamma}
, equation (
121
) holds without the second term in its right-hand side, while in the case
\alpha=\sqrt{\gamma}
, we have a half of the second term.

The remaining summand of (
120
) can be written as

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!\gamma^{k-m-1}}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}u^{m}}(u^{k-1})\right|_{u=1}=\frac{1}{4\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma}}}\frac{1}{1+z}\left(\gamma z+\frac{1}{z}+\gamma+1\right)^{k}d\text{}z

=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\gamma+1-2\sqrt{\gamma}}^{\gamma+1+2\sqrt{\gamma}}\frac{t+1-\gamma}{t}\frac{t^{k}}{\sqrt{(\gamma+1+2\sqrt{\gamma}-t)(t-\gamma-1+2\sqrt{\gamma})}}d\text{}t.

Combining the formulas, we obtain that the correction measure is equal to

\left(\frac{\alpha(t-2\alpha-\gamma-1)}{\gamma+\alpha^{2}+\alpha\gamma+\alpha-\alpha t}-\frac{t+1-\gamma}{2t}\right)\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{(\gamma+1+2\sqrt{\gamma}-t)(t-\gamma-1+2\sqrt{\gamma})}}\mathbf{1}_{t\in[(\sqrt{\gamma}-1)^{2};(\sqrt{\gamma}+1)^{2}]}\\
+\delta\left(\alpha+1+\gamma+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\alpha>\sqrt{\gamma}}+\frac{1}{2}\delta\left(\alpha+1+\gamma+\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\alpha=\sqrt{\gamma}}.

(122)

This answer (see also Figure
2
) illustrates a discrete analog of the BBP phase transition for random matrices. We see that if one perturbs the large one-sided Plancherel representation (discrete analog of GUE) by multiplying its character to a character of one-dimensional representation, the resulting limiting measure has an outlier in the case
\alpha>\sqrt{\gamma}
, and does not have it otherwise. This is analogous to a random matrix phenomenon.

Figure 2:
The density from (
122
) for (
\gamma=9
,
\alpha=2
) and (
\gamma=9
,
\alpha=4
), respectively. It is visible that for
\alpha=2
the correction measure created by a perturbation is positive at the right of its support and negative at the left. For
\alpha=4
, the outlier is created, thus the correction measure is negative inside the support of its continuous part.

Example 7

In this example we will address the perturbation of the model of uniformly random domino tilings of the Aztec diamond. Let us introduce very briefly the required notions. We refer to
[
BK
]
for a detailed description of the construction below.

A signature
\lambda
with non-negative entries can be also thought of as a Young diagram and vice versa. Two Young diagrams
\lambda,\mu
interlace (notation
\mu\prec\lambda
) if
\lambda_{1}\geq\mu_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}\geq\dots
holds. Two Young diagrams interlace vertically
(notation
\mu\prec_{v}\lambda
) if the conjugations of
\lambda
and
\mu
interlace in the sense defined above.
By
|\lambda|
one denotes the sum
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\dots
, which is the total number of boxes in the Young diagram
\lambda
.

It is known that domino tilings of the Aztec diamond of size
M
are in a natural bijection with the collection of Young diagrams
(\lambda^{1},\mu^{1},\dots,\lambda^{M})
satisfying

\varnothing\prec\lambda^{1}\succ_{v}\mu^{1}\prec\lambda^{2}\succ_{v}\mu^{2}\prec\dots\prec\lambda^{M}\succ_{v}\varnothing.

Each signature
\lambda^{r}
describes the positions of domino tilings along a one-dimensional slice of the Aztec diamond.

One can introduce a probability measure on domino tilings of the Aztec diamond in various ways. The first one is to consider the uniform measure on all tilings. Uniformly random domino tilings were extensively studied in the last thirty years, in particular, in pioneering works
[
JPS
]
,
[
J
]
.

We will consider a perturbation of the uniform measure. Let us view the Aztec diamond as a square, and attach to horizontal dominoes along the first (top) up-right row (if the Aztec diamond is depicted as in Figure
1
) the weight
A>1
, while all the other dominoes have weight 1. Now, consider a measure on the space of domino tilings with the probability of a tiling being proportional to

A^{\text{number of horizontal dominoes in the first up-right row}}.

In terms of interlacing Young diagrams above, the probability
ZA^{|\lambda^{M}|}
is attached to a sequence
(\lambda^{1},\mu^{1},\dots,\lambda^{M})
, where
Z
is a normaizing constant.

For
1\leq N\leq M
, it follows from
[
BK
, Sections 2 and 8]
that the Schur generating function of the (marginal) distribution of the signature
\lambda^{N}
under such a measure is given by

\prod_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{1+x_{i}}{2}\right)^{M-N-1}\frac{1+Ax_{i}}{1+A}.

To make formulas below more readable, we assume that
N=\alpha M
exactly (without rounding up or down), where
0<\alpha<1
is fixed. Then, as
N\to\infty
, we can apply Theorem
14
in order to study the asymptotic behavior of the signature
\lambda^{N}
: we see that the assumptions of the theorem hold with

\Psi(x)=\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1\right)\log\left(\frac{1+x}{2}\right),\qquad\Phi(x)=\log\left(\frac{1+Ax}{1+x}\right).

Let us assume additionally that
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
. Performing very similar to the previous example and somewhat lengthy computations (we omit them for brevity), one arrives to the following formula for the correction measure:

-\mathbf{1}_{\alpha>\frac{(A+1)^{2}}{2(A^{2}+1)}}\delta\left(\frac{-1+2\alpha A-A}{\alpha(A^{2}-1)}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}_{\alpha=\frac{(A+1)^{2}}{2(A^{2}+1)}}\delta\left(\frac{-1+2\alpha A-A}{\alpha(A^{2}-1)}\right)\\
+\left(\alpha+\frac{-2\alpha+1}{4y}-\frac{\alpha(2\alpha-1)}{4(\alpha y-1)}+\frac{\alpha(2A^{2}\alpha-A^{2}-2A+2\alpha-1)}{2(-\alpha y+A^{2}\alpha y-2\alpha A+1+A)}\right)\frac{\mathbf{1}_{1-(2\alpha-1)^{2}-(2\alpha y-1)^{2}>0}}{\pi\sqrt{1-(2\alpha-1)^{2}-(2\alpha y-1)^{2}}}dy.

(123)

As discussed in Section
1
, the result explains geometric properties of a random tiling visible on Figure
1
, and also it is related to another setup known as the Tangent Method for models of statistical mechanics. Formula (
18
) can be obtained from the formula for moments in a standard way. Note that in our assumptions (in particular,
\alpha>1/2
) outside of the arctic curve we have (the maximal possible) density 1 of particles encoded by a signature; therefore, the outlier is parameterized by a delta measure with a negative sign.

Remark 5

All general theorems in the current paper provide formulas for moments of limiting (correction) measures. In order to find their density, in all examples we perform direct calculations for finding the integral representation for moments. In this remark we outline an another approach – via Stieltjes transforms – which is comparable in complexity and can be more convenient in some other setups. We will focus on the statement of Theorem
14
, a similar computation can be performed for other theorems from this paper as well.

In notations of Theorem
14
, using the binomial theorem, one gets

\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{k}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint\left(\Phi^{\prime}(x)-\frac{1}{2x}\right)\mathcal{F}(x)^{k}dx,

where
\mathcal{F}(x):=x\Psi^{\prime}(x)+\frac{x}{x-1}
, and the contour of integration encircles

x=1
