and no other poles of the integrand. Therefore, we have

-\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{m}^{\prime}_{k}y^{k+1}=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint\frac{\Phi^{\prime}(x)-\frac{1}{2x}}{y}\frac{1}{1-\frac{\mathcal{F}(x)}{y}}dx.

(124)

The equation
\mathcal{F}(x)=y
has a unique solution
x_{+}(y)
for large
|y|
(this follows from the formal power series technique). The solution can be analytically continued to the upper half-plane. In examples, this continuation often can be given explicitly, and it is also continuous on a real line (though we do not know how to justify these steps in full generality). In such a case, the contour integral in (
124
) can be calculated via computing the residue at the single pole inside the contour of integration for large
|y|
, and then analytically continued.
One obtains the following formula for the Stieltjes transform of the correction measure:

St_{\mu}(y)=\frac{\Phi^{\prime}(x_{+}(y))-\frac{1}{2x_{+}(y)}}{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(x_{+}(y))},\qquad\Im(y)>0.

Thus, the formula for the density of the correction measure is given by

d(y)=\frac{1}{\pi}\Im\left(\frac{\Phi^{\prime}(x_{+}(y))-\frac{1}{2x_{+}(y)}}{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(x_{+}(y))}\right),\qquad y\in\mathbb{R},

while outliers can be found from the condition
\Phi^{\prime}(x_{+}(y))=\infty
.

7
Second order infinitesimal free probability

In this section we analyze the third leading term in the expectation of the empirical measure of random matrices via its Harish-Chandra transform. We also connect it with notions coming from second order infinitesimal free probability.

The main result of the section is the following theorem.

Theorem 16

Let
A
be a random Hermitian matrix of size
N
and assume that for every
finite
r
one has

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{2}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{E}[H\text{}C(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},0^{N-r};\lambda_{1}(A),\ldots,\lambda_{N}(A))]-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\Psi(x_{i})-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\Phi(x_{i})\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathrm{T}(x_{i}),

(125)

where
\Psi,\Phi,\mathrm{T}
are smooth functions in a complex neighborhood of
0
and
the above convergence is uniform in a complex neighborhood of
0^{r}
. Then the
1/N^{2}
correction of the average empirical distribution of
N^{-1}\cdot A

is given by

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{2}\left(\frac{1}{N^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}(A^{k})]-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}-\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{k}\right)=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}+\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime\prime}_{k},

where
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}},(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are given by (
54
),(
81
) respectively. The sequences

(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}},(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime\prime}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are given by

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}=\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-1}\mathrm{T}^{\prime}(x))\right|_{x=0}

\text{{\hskip 102.22168pt}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m=0}^{k-2}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-2)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-2}(\Phi^{\prime}(x))^{2})\right|_{x=0}

(126)

and

\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}=\frac{1}{24}\sum_{m=0}^{k-3}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-3)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-3}\Psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(x))\right|_{x=0}

\text{{\hskip 40.88867pt}}+\frac{1}{12}\sum_{m=0}^{k-3}\frac{k!}{m!(m+2)!(k-m-3)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-3}\Psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(x))\right|_{x=0}

\text{{\hskip 102.22168pt}}+\frac{1}{12}\sum_{m=0}^{k-4}\frac{k!}{m!(m+2)!(k-m-4)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-4}(\Psi^{\prime\prime}(x))^{2})\right|_{x=0}.

(127)

Proof.

For notation simplicity we write
f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})=\mathbb{E}[H\text{}C(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\lambda_{1}(A),\ldots,\lambda_{N}(A))]
. Note
that (
125
) implies that the relations (
40
),(
79
)
are satisfied. Thus, the empirical distribution of
N^{-1}\cdot A

converges to a probability measure with moments

(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and its
1/N
correction is given
by
(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. We have also shown an
expansion

\frac{1}{N^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}(A^{k})]=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\frac{1}{N^{i}}M_{i,N}(\Psi),

(128)

where
N^{2}M_{0,N}(\Psi)
is a linear combination of terms of the form

N^{2}\partial_{i_{m}}\ldots\partial_{i_{1}}\left(\partial_{i_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{k-m}(0^{N}).

(129)

Due to (
125
), for
|\{i_{0},\ldots,i_{m}\}|>1
, (
129
)
converges to
0
as
N\rightarrow\infty
, which implies that

N^{2}\left(M_{0,N}(\Psi)-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}-\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{k}\right)=\omicron(1)+N^{2}\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m)!}\times

\left(\left.\partial_{1}^{m}\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{k-m}(0^{N})-\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m})\right|_{x=0}-\frac{k-m}{N}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-1}\Phi^{\prime}(x))\right|_{x=0}\right).

For fixed
m\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}
, by Leibniz rule, we get

N^{2}\left(\partial_{1}^{m}\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{k-m}(0^{N})-\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m})\right|_{x=0}-\frac{k-m}{N}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-1}\Phi^{\prime}(x))\right|_{x=0}\right)

=\sum_{l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}=m}\frac{m!}{l_{1}!\ldots l_{k-m}!}

\times\left(N^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{k-m}\partial_{1}^{l_{i}+1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)(0^{N})-N^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{k-m}\Psi^{(l_{i}+1)}(0)-N\sum_{i=1}^{k-m}\Phi^{(l_{i}+1)}(0)\prod_{\underset{j\neq i}{j=1}}^{k-m}\Psi^{(l_{j}+1)}(0)\right)

(130)

For fixed indices
l_{1},\ldots,l_{k-m}
, due to (
125
), for
every
i\in\{1,\ldots,k-m\}
, we have

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{2}\left.\partial_{1}^{l_{i}+1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)(0^{N})-N^{2}\Psi^{(l_{i}+1)}(0)-N\Phi^{(l_{i}+1)}(0)\right)=\mathrm{T}^{(l_{i}+1)}(0),

and by induction on
k-m
we obtain

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\left(N^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{k-m}\partial_{1}^{l_{i}+1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)(0^{N})-N^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{k-m}\Psi^{(l_{i}+1)}(0)-N\sum_{i=1}^{k-m}\Phi^{(l_{i}+1)}(0)\prod_{\underset{j\neq i}{j=1}}^{k-m}\Psi^{(l_{j}+1)}(0)\right)

=\sum_{i=1}^{k-m}\mathrm{T}^{(l_{i}+1)}(0)\prod_{\underset{j\neq i}{j=1}}^{k-m}\Psi^{(l_{j}+1)}(0)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\underset{i\neq j}{i,j=1}}^{k-m}\Phi^{(l_{i}+1)}(0)\Phi^{(l_{j}+1)}(0)\prod_{\underset{r\neq i,j}{r=1}}^{k-m}\Psi^{(l_{r}+1)}(0).

Therefore, as
N\rightarrow\infty
, (
130
) converges to

(k-m)\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-1}\mathrm{T}^{\prime}(x))\right|_{x=0}+\frac{(k-m)\text{}(k-m-1)}{2}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-2}(\Phi^{\prime}(x))^{2})\right|_{x=0},

which implies that

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{2}\left(M_{0,N}(\Psi)-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}-\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{k}\right)=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}.

Thus, the claim holds if
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(N\text{}\cdot M_{1,N}(\Psi)+M_{2,N}(\Psi))=\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}
.
The computation of
M_{1,N}(\Psi)
was presented in the proof of Theorem
12
.
Taking also into account the assumption (
125
), we have

N\cdot M_{1,N}(\Psi)=\omicron(1)+\frac{N}{2}\sum_{m=0}^{k-2}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-2)!}\partial_{1}^{m}\left(\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{k-m-2}\partial_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)(0^{N})

-\frac{N}{2}\sum_{m=1}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{(m-1)!m!(k-m)!}\partial_{1}^{m}\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{k-m}(0^{N})=\omicron(1).

Therefore,
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N\cdot M_{1,N}(\Psi)=0
. In order to
determine
M_{2,N}(\Psi)
, note that relation
(
40
) implies that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}(A^{k})]
can be written as a linear combination of terms

N^{l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}}\binom{N}{m+1}c_{N}(m),

(131)

where
m\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}
,
l_{1}+2l_{2}+\cdots+(k-m)l_{k-m}=k-m
, and
c_{N}(m)
’s are linear combinations of derivatives of the form
(
49
). Then,
M_{2,N}(\Psi)
is given by the sum of the summands of
terms (
131
) with factor
N^{k-1}
. There are three cases that we have to
consider regarding
l_{1},\ldots,l_{k-m}
in order to compute these summands, since a term

N^{l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}}\binom{N}{m+1}

should be a polynomial in
N
of degree at least
k-1
. Thus, we must have
l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}\in\{k-m,k-m-1,k-m-2\}
, since a
binomial coefficient is a polynomial in
N
of degree
m+1
. For each
case, we are interested in the coefficient of
N^{k-1}
. For
l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}=k-m-1
, i.e.
l_{1}=k-m-2
,
l_{2}=1
and
l_{3}=\cdots=l_{k-m}=0
the summands of the terms (
131
) with factor

N^{k-1}
are

-N^{k-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}i\right)\left(\frac{k!}{2m!(m+1)!(k-m-2)!}\partial_{1}^{m}\left(\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{k-m-2}\partial_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)(0^{N})+\omicron(1)\right)

=-N^{k-1}\left(\frac{k!}{4(m-1)!m!(k-m-2)!}\partial_{1}^{m}\left(\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{k-m-2}\partial_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)(0^{N})+\omicron(1)\right),

where
m\in\{1,\ldots,k-2\}
. Thus, the contribution of these summands
to
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}M_{2,N}(\Psi)
will be

-\sum_{m=0}^{k-3}\frac{k!}{4m!(m+1)!(k-m-3)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m+1}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m+1}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-3}\Psi^{\prime\prime}(x))\right|_{x=0}.

(132)

For the case
l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}=k-m-2
we have that
l_{1}=k-m-4
,
l_{2}=2
,
l_{3}=\cdots=l_{k-m}=0
or
l_{1}=k-m-3
,
l_{3}=1
and
l_{2}=l_{4}=\cdots=l_{k-m}=0
. For
l_{1}=k-m-4
,
l_{2}=2
and
l_{3}=\cdots=l_{k-m}=0
, the summands of the terms (
131
)
with factor
N^{k-1}
are

N^{k-1}\left(\frac{k!}{8m!(m+1)!(k-m-4)!}\partial_{1}^{m}\left(\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{k-m-4}\left(\partial_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{2}\right)(0^{N})+\omicron(1)\right),

where
m\in\{0,\ldots,k-4\}
. On the other hand, for
l_{1}=k-m-3
,
l_{3}=1
and
l_{2}=l_{4}=\cdots=l_{k-m}=0
, the summands of the
terms (
131
) with factor
N^{k-1}
are

N^{k-1}\left(\frac{k!}{6m!(m+1)!(k-m-3)!}\partial_{1}^{m}\left(\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{k-m-3}\partial_{1}^{3}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)(0^{N})+\omicron(1)\right),

where
m\in\{0,\ldots,k-3\}
. Hence, the case
l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}=k-m-2
contributes to
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}M_{2,N}(\Psi)

the expression

\sum_{m=0}^{k-4}\frac{k!}{8m!(m+1)!(k-m-4)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-4}(\Psi^{\prime\prime}(x))^{2})\right|_{x=0}

\text{{\hskip 100.00015pt}}+\sum_{m=0}^{k-3}\frac{k!}{6m!(m+1)!(k-m-3)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-3}\Psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(x))\right|_{x=0}.

(133)

For the last case
l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}=k-m
, we have
l_{1}=k-m
and
l_{2}=\cdots=l_{k-m}=0
. Then, the desired summands will be

N^{k-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\underset{i\neq j}{i,j=0}}^{m}i\text{}j\right)\left(\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m)!}\partial_{1}^{m}\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{k-m}(0^{N})+\omicron(1)\right)

\text{{\hskip 40.00006pt}}=N^{k-1}\frac{k!}{8(m-2)!(m-1)!(k-m)!}\partial_{1}^{m}\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{k-m}(0^{N})

\text{{\hskip 80.00012pt}}+N^{k-1}\frac{k!}{12(m-2)!m!(k-m)!}\partial_{1}^{m}\left(\partial_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f\right)\right)^{k-m}(0^{N})+N^{k-1}\omicron(1),

where
m\in\{2,\ldots,k-1\}
. Thus, the contribution of these summands
to
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}M_{2,N}(\Psi)
will be equal to

\sum_{m=0}^{k-3}\left(\frac{k!}{8m!(m+1)!(k-m-2)!}+\frac{k!}{12m!(m+2)!(k-m-2)!}\right)\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m+2}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m+2}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-2})\right|_{x=0}.

(134)

Since
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}M_{2,N}(\Psi)
is equal to the sum of
(
132
),(
133
) and (
134
), a straightforward computation shows
that the limit is equal to
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}
. This proves the claim.
∎

We would like to better understand the terms

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k},\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}
from Theorem
16
.
In the proof of
Theorem
16
we showed that
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}M_{2,N}(\Psi)
, and that
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}
contributes
to the
1/N^{2}
correction of
A/N
due to the relation

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{2}\left(M_{0,N}(\Psi)-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}-\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{k}\right).

However, we would like to provide also a different explanation of the term
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}
,
which makes the connection with second order infinitesimal free probability.
This connection is a generalization of the fact that the formula (
81
) for the

1/N
correction of
A/N
gives an explicit description for the
infinitesimal free cumulants of the infinitesimal non-commutative probability
space
(\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle,\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})
, where
for every
P\in\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle

\varphi(P)=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}\text{}P(N^{-1}\cdot A)]\text{\quad and\quad}\varphi^{\prime}(P)=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N\left(\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}\text{}P(N^{-1}\cdot A)]-\varphi(P)\right).

By the above equalities,
\mu_{k}=\varphi(\mathbf{x}^{k})
and

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{k}=\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{k})
, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
The role of functions
\Psi,\Phi
in the computation of infinitesimal free
cumulants can be seen from (
90
) and (
91
). Thus, thinking of

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{k}
as moments, we have that the
relations (
54
),(
81
) describe moment-cumulant relations for an infinitesimal non-commutative probability space of order 1. The fact that
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}
is determined by (
126
) allows us to go a step further and see the relations
(
54
),(
81
),(
126
) as moment-cumulant relations, in the sense
of Definition
4
, for an infinitesimal non-commutative probability space of order
2. This is shown by the next lemma.

Lemma 7

Let
\Psi,\Phi,\mathrm{T}
be infinitely differentiable functions at
0
and
consider the sequences
(c_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(c^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(c^{\prime\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, where for every
n\in\mathbb{N}

c_{n}:=\frac{\Psi^{(n)}(0)}{(n-1)!},\text{\hskip 10.22217pt}c^{\prime}_{n}:=\frac{\Phi^{(n)}(0)}{(n-1)!}\text{\hskip 10.22217ptand\hskip 10.22217pt}c^{\prime\prime}_{n}:=2\frac{\mathrm{T}^{(n)}(0)}{(n-1)!}.

Then for the sequence
(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
defined by
(
126
), for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{\pi\in\operatorname{NC}(k)}\sum_{V\in\pi}c^{\prime\prime}_{|V|}\prod_{\underset{W\neq V}{W\in\pi}}c_{|W|}+\sum_{\pi\in\operatorname{NC}(k)}\sum_{\underset{W\neq V}{V,W\in\pi}}c^{\prime}_{|V|}c^{\prime}_{|W|}\prod_{\underset{U\neq V,W}{U\in\pi}}c_{|U|}\right).

Proof.

In Lemma
5
we proved, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, that

\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-1}\mathrm{T}^{\prime}(x))\right|_{x=0}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\pi\in\operatorname{NC}(k)}\sum_{V\in\pi}c^{\prime\prime}_{|V|}\prod_{\underset{W\neq V}{W\in\pi}}c_{|W|}.

Thus, it suffices to show that

\sum_{m=0}^{k-2}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-2)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}(x))^{k-m-2}(\Phi^{\prime}(x))^{2})\right|_{x=0}=\sum_{\pi\in\operatorname{NC}(k)}\sum_{\underset{W\neq V}{V,W\in\pi}}c^{\prime}_{|V|}c^{\prime}_{|W|}\prod_{\underset{U\neq V,W}{U\in\pi}}c_{|U|}.

(135)

By Leibniz rule, the left hand side of (
135
) is equal to

\sum_{m=0}^{k-2}\sum_{l_{1+\cdots+l_{k-m}=m}}\frac{k!}{(m+1)!(k-m-2)!\prod_{i=1}^{k-m}l_{i}!}\Phi^{(l_{k-m}+1)}(0)\Phi^{(l_{k-m-1}+1)}(0)\prod_{i=1}^{k-m-2}\Psi^{(l_{i}+1)}(0).

(136)

The way that we related
(k-m)
-tuples
(l_{1},\ldots,l_{k-m})
such
that
l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k-m}=m
with non-crossing partitions
\pi=\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{k-m}\}\in\operatorname{NC}(k)
in Lemma
5

will lead to the equality (
135
). In more detail, for
m\in\{0,\ldots,k-2\}
, consider non-negative integers
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k-m}
such that
\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{k-m}=m
. We also assume that
\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k-m}\}=\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{a}\}
, where
\nu_{i}\neq\nu_{j}
, for every
i\neq j
, and
we define for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,a\}

r_{i}:=|\{j=1,\ldots,k-m:\lambda_{j}=\nu_{i}\}|.

We would like to calculate the contribution of
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k-m})
to the latter sum of (
136
). In order to do so, we
consider two cases. If we assume
l_{k-m}\neq l_{k-m-1}
, the
(k-m)
-tuple
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k-m})

contributes terms of the form

\frac{k!}{(m+1)!(k-m-2)!\prod_{t=1}^{k-m}\lambda_{t}!}\Phi^{(\nu_{i}+1)}(0)\Phi^{(\nu_{j}+1)}(0)(\Psi^{(\nu_{i}+1)}(0))^{r_{i}-1}(\Psi^{(\nu_{j}+1)}(0))^{r_{j}-1}\prod_{\underset{t\neq i,j}{t=1}}^{a}(\Psi^{(\nu_{t}+1)}(0))^{r_{t}},

where
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,a\}
, with
i<j
. The number of times that
such a term will appear in the latter sum of (
136
) is equal to the
number of different ways that we can cover
k-m-2
points on a line
segment with
r_{t}
number of elements
\nu_{t}
(
t\in\{1,\ldots,a\}\backslash\{i,j\}
),
r_{i}-1
number of elements
\nu_{i}
and
r_{j}-1

number of elements
\nu_{j}
. This is equal to

C_{i,j}:=\frac{(k-m-2)!}{r_{1}!\ldots r_{i-1}!(r_{i}-1)!r_{i+1}!\ldots r_{j-1}!(r_{j}-1)!r_{j+1}!\ldots r_{a}!}.

Thus, we see that the
(k-m)
-tuple
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k-m})

contributes to the latter sum of (
136
) the expression

2\frac{k!}{(m+1)!(k-m-2)!\prod_{t=1}^{a}(\nu_{t}!)^{r_{t}}}\sum_{\underset{i<j}{i,j=1}}^{a}C_{i,j}\Phi^{(\nu_{i}+1)}(0)\Phi^{(\nu_{j}+1)}(0)(\Psi^{(\nu_{i}+1)}(0))^{r_{i}-1}(\Psi^{(\nu_{j}+1)}(0))^{r_{j}-1}\prod_{\underset{t\neq i,j}{t=1}}^{a}(\Psi^{(\nu_{t}+1)}(0))^{r_{t}}.

By the definition of
(c_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(c^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

this is equal to

\frac{k!}{(m+1)!\prod_{i=1}^{a}r_{i}!}\sum_{\underset{i\neq j}{i,j=1}}^{a}r_{i}r_{j}c^{\prime}_{\nu_{i}+1}c^{\prime}_{\nu_{j}+1}(c_{\nu_{i}+1})^{r_{i}-1}(c_{\nu_{j}+1})^{r_{j}-1}\prod_{\underset{t\neq i,j}{t=1}}^{a}(c_{\nu_{t}+1})^{r_{t}}.

(137)

In the second case, for
l_{k-m}=l_{k-m-1}
, the contributions of

(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k-m})
to the latter sum of (
136
)
will be of the form

\frac{k!}{(m+1)!(k-m-2)!\prod_{i=1}^{k-m}\lambda_{i}!}(\Phi^{(\nu_{j}+1)}(0))^{2}(\Psi^{(\nu_{j}+1)}(0))^{r_{j}-2}\prod_{\underset{i\neq j}{i=1}}^{a}(\Psi^{(\nu_{i}+1)}(0))^{r_{i}},

for some
j\in\{1,\ldots,a\}
. The number of times that this term will
appear in the latter sum of (
136
) is equal to the number of
different ways that we can cover
k-m-2
points on a line segment with

r_{i}
number of elements
\nu_{i}
(
i\in\{1,\ldots,a\}\backslash\{j\}
)
and
r_{j}-2
number of elements
\nu_{j}
, i.e.

\frac{(k-m-2)!}{r_{1}!\ldots r_{j-1}!(r_{j}-2)!r_{j+1}!\ldots r_{a}!}.

Thus,
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k-m})
will further contribute to the
latter sum of (
136
) the expression

\frac{k!}{(m+1)!\prod_{i=1}^{a}(\nu_{i}!)^{r_{i}}}\sum_{i=1}^{a}\frac{(\Phi^{(\nu_{i}+1)}(0))^{2}(\Psi^{(\nu_{i}+1)}(0))^{r_{i}-2}}{r_{1}!\ldots r_{i-1}!(r_{i}-2)!r_{i+1}!\ldots r_{a}!}\prod_{\underset{j\neq i}{j=1}}^{a}(\Psi^{(\nu_{j}+1)}(0))^{r_{j}}

=\frac{k!}{(m+1)!\prod_{i=1}^{a}r_{i}!}\sum_{i=1}^{a}r_{i}(r_{i}-1)(c^{\prime}_{\nu_{i}+1})^{2}(c_{\nu_{i}+1})^{r_{i}-2}\prod_{\underset{j\neq i}{j=1}}^{a}(c_{\nu_{j}+1})^{r_{j}}.

(138)

The total contribution of
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k-m})
is given by
the sum of (
137
) and (
138
).

Regarding the right hand
side of (
135
), a partition
\pi=\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{k-m}\}\in\operatorname{NC}(k)
which has
r_{1}
blocks with
\nu_{1}+1

elements,…,
r_{a}
blocks with
\nu_{a}+1
elements, contributes

\sum_{\underset{i\neq j}{i,j=1}}^{k-m}c^{\prime}_{|V_{i}|}c^{\prime}_{|V_{j}|}\prod_{\underset{t\neq i,j}{t=1}}^{k-m}c_{|V_{t}|}=\sum_{\underset{i\neq j\text{ and }|V_{i}|=|V_{j}|}{i,j=1}}^{k-m}c^{\prime}_{|V_{i}|}c^{\prime}_{|V_{j}|}\prod_{\underset{t\neq i,j}{t=1}}^{k-m}c_{|V_{t}|}+\sum_{\underset{|V_{i}|\neq|V_{j}|}{i,j=1}}^{k-m}c^{\prime}_{|V_{i}|}c^{\prime}_{|V_{j}|}\prod_{\underset{t\neq i,j}{t=1}}^{k-m}c_{|V_{t}|}

=\sum_{i=1}^{a}r_{i}(r_{i}-1)(c^{\prime}_{\nu_{i}+1})^{2}(c_{\nu_{i}+1})^{r_{i}-2}\prod_{\underset{j\neq i}{j=1}}^{a}(c_{\nu_{j}+1})^{r_{j}}+\sum_{\underset{i\neq j}{i,j=1}}^{a}r_{i}r_{j}c^{\prime}_{\nu_{i}+1}c^{\prime}_{\nu_{j}+1}(c_{\nu_{i}+1})^{r_{i}-1}(c_{\nu_{j}+1})^{r_{j}-1}\prod_{\underset{t\neq i,j}{t=1}}^{a}(c_{\nu_{t}+1})^{r_{t}}.

Since the number of such partitions is equal to
\left((m+1)!\prod_{i=1}^{a}r_{i}!\right)^{-1}k!
, this proves (
135
) and the claim
holds.
∎

In the remainder of this section we make some explicit computations, which
illustrate how second order fluctuations of random matrix models are described by certain signed measures on
\mathbb{R}
. Our models consist of additive
perturbations of ergodic unitarily invariant matrices.

Example 8

We consider the Hermitian random matrix of size
N
,

A=N\cdot A_{1}+\sqrt{N}A_{2}+A_{3}
, where
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
are
independent GUE matrices. As a consequence, for every
H\in H(N)
, one has

\mathbb{E}[\exp(\operatorname{Tr}(H\text{}A))]=\prod_{x\in\operatorname{Spec}(H)}\exp\left(N\frac{x^{2}}{2}+\frac{x^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{N}\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right).

The
1/N^{2}
correction of the matrix
A_{1}
is given by

\frac{1}{12}\sum_{m=0}^{k-4}\frac{k!}{m!(m+2)!(k-m-4)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}(x^{k-m-4})\right|_{x=0}=\frac{k(k-1)}{24\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{m=0}^{k-4}\binom{k-2}{m+2}\oint_{|z|=1}\frac{z^{k-m-4}}{z^{m+1}}d\text{}z

=\frac{k(k-1)}{24\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}z^{k-1}\sum_{m=2}^{k-2}\binom{k-2}{m}\left(\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right)^{m}d\text{}z=\frac{k(k-1)}{24\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}z\left(z+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k-2}d\text{}z

for every
k\geq 4
. Using the change of variables
u=2\cos\text{}t

in order to compute the last contour integral, we see that the second order
correction functional of
A_{1}

\phi^{\prime\prime}(P):=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{2}\left(\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}\text{}P(A_{1})]-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}P(t)\sqrt{4-t^{2}}d\text{}t\right)\text{, \quad}P\in\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle,

is equal to

\phi^{\prime\prime}(P)=\frac{1}{24\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}P^{\prime\prime}(t)\frac{t^{2}-2}{\sqrt{4-t^{2}}}d\text{}t,\text{\quad for every }P\in\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle.

A similar formula for the second order correction functional of
A_{1}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}A_{2}+\frac{1}{N}A_{3}
can be derived if we consider an
integral representation for the terms that correspond to the
infinitesimal non-commutative probability space of order
2
. One of this
terms is

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m=0}^{k-2}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-2)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}(x^{k-m})\right|_{x=0}=\frac{k}{4\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{m=0}^{k-2}\binom{k-1}{m+1}\oint_{|z|=1}\frac{z^{k-m}}{z^{m+1}}d\text{}z

=\frac{k}{4\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}z^{k+1}\sum_{m=1}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{m}\left(\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right)^{m}d\text{}z=\frac{k}{4\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}z^{2}\left(z+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k-1}d\text{}z,

for every
k\geq 2
. The remaining term is the first order correction of

A_{1}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}A_{2}
and it was studied in Example
2
. Concluding,
the second order correction functional
\varphi^{\prime\prime}
of
A_{1}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}A_{2}+\frac{1}{N}A_{3}
is given by

\varphi^{\prime\prime}(P)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}\left(P^{\prime\prime}(t)\frac{t^{2}-2}{12}+P^{\prime}(t)\frac{t^{3}-3t}{2}+P(t)(t^{2}-2)\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{4-t^{2}}}d\text{}t,\text{\quad for every }P\in\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle.

Example 9

We consider the random matrix

A=N\cdot G+\theta_{1}X\text{}X^{\ast}+\frac{\theta_{2}}{N}Y\text{}Y^{\ast},

(139)

where the matrices
G,X,Y
are independent,
G
is a GUE of size
N
and

X,Y
are independent
N\times 1
vectors with standard complex Gaussian i.i.d. entries. Then, in notation of Theorem
16
,
we have that

\Psi(x)=\frac{x^{2}}{2},\text{{\hskip 30.00005pt}}\Phi(x)=\log(1-\theta_{1}x)^{-1},\text{{\hskip 30.00005pt}}\mathrm{T}(x)=\theta_{2}x.

Applying the theorem and performing very similar to previous examples calculations, one arrives to the following answer for the corrections.
Consider the infinitesimal non-commutative probability space of order 2,

(\mathbb{C}\langle X\rangle,\varphi^{(0)},\varphi^{(1)},\varphi^{(2)})
, where
\varphi^{(0)}
is the limit,
\varphi^{(1)}
is the
first order correction and
\varphi^{(2)}
is the second order correction.
Then we have that

\varphi^{(1)}(P)=\boldsymbol{1}_{|\theta_{1}|\geq 1}\cdot P(\theta_{1}+\theta_{1}^{-1})-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}\frac{t-2\theta_{1}}{(t-\theta_{1}-\theta_{1}^{-1})\sqrt{4-t^{2}}}P(t)d\text{}t,

and

\varphi^{(2)}(P)=\frac{\theta_{2}}{2\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}\sqrt{4-t^{2}}P^{\prime}(t)d\text{}t+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}\frac{\theta_{1}^{2}}{\sqrt{4-t^{2}}}\frac{P^{\prime}(t)-P^{\prime}(\theta_{1}+\theta^{-1}_{1})}{t-\theta_{1}-\theta_{1}^{-1}}d\text{}t

-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}\frac{(\theta_{1}^{2}-1)(t-2\theta_{1})}{(t-\theta_{1}-\theta_{1}^{-1})\sqrt{4-t^{2}}}\left(\frac{P^{\prime}(t)-P^{\prime}(\theta_{1}+\theta_{1}^{-1})}{t-\theta_{1}-\theta_{1}^{-1}}-P^{\prime\prime}(\theta_{1}+\theta_{1}^{-1})\right)d\text{}t+\frac{1}{24\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}P^{\prime\prime}(t)\frac{t^{2}-2}{\sqrt{4-t^{2}}}d\text{}t.

Instead of
\frac{\theta_{2}}{N}Y\text{}Y^{\ast}
in (
139
), one can
consider the deterministic Hermitian matrix with one non-zero eigenvalue

\theta_{2}
. This would lead to the same result for corrections.

8
Higher order infinitesimal free probability

In this section we present our most general result, focusing on corrections of arbitrary order
of certain random matrices. Our goal is to provide explicit
computations that allow us to make the connection with non-crossing cumulant
functionals of
k
-th order.
As before, we are interested in an asymptotic expansion

\frac{1}{N^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}(A^{k})]=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\frac{1}{N^{i}}M_{i,N}(\Psi),

(140)

where
(M_{i,N}(\Psi))_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges for every
i
. We would like to understand under which further conditions on
A
one can control the terms

M_{i,N}(\Psi)
, for
i\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, as
N\rightarrow\infty
.

Theorem 17

Let
n
be a fixed integer. Let
A
be a random Hermitian matrix of size
N
and assume
that for every finite
r
one has

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{n\varepsilon}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{E}[H\text{}C(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},0^{N-r};\lambda_{1}(A),\ldots,\lambda_{n}(A))]-\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\sum_{i=0}^{r}\frac{1}{N^{j\varepsilon}}\Psi_{j}(x_{i})\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\Psi_{n}(x_{i}),

(141)

where
\Psi_{0},\ldots,\Psi_{n}
are smooth functions in a complex neighborhood of

0
, the above convergence is uniform in a complex neighborhood of
0^{r}
and
0<\varepsilon<n^{-1}
. Then the
1/N^{n\varepsilon}
correction of the
average empirical distribution of
N^{-1}A
is given by

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{n\varepsilon}\left(\frac{1}{N^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}(A^{k})]-\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{N^{j\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)}_{k}\right)=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{(n)},

(142)

where for every
j\in\{0,\ldots,n\}

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(j)}_{k}:=\sum_{b_{1}+2b_{2}+\cdots+j\text{}b_{j}=j}\sum_{m=0}^{k-b_{1}-\cdots-b_{j}}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-b_{1}-\cdots-b_{j})!b_{1}!\ldots b_{j}!}\text{{\hskip 81.77734pt}}

\text{{\hskip 143.11035pt}}\times\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}_{0}(x))^{k-m-b_{1}-\cdots-b_{j}}(\Psi^{\prime}_{1}(x))^{b_{1}}\ldots(\Psi^{\prime}_{j}(x))^{b_{j}})\right|_{x=0}.

(143)

Proof.

First, we consider an easier case, in which we additionally assume that

N^{n\varepsilon}\left(\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{E}[H\text{}C(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\lambda_{1}(A),\ldots,\lambda_{N}(\mathrm{A}))]-\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\sum_{i=0}^{N}\frac{1}{N^{j\varepsilon}}\Psi_{j}(x_{i})\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Psi_{n}(x_{i}),

(144)

for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus,
denoting the average Harish-Chandra integral of
A
by
f_{N}
, we have that

f_{N}=f_{0}f_{1}\ldots f_{n}
, where

f_{j}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})=\exp\left(N^{1-j\varepsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Psi_{j}(x_{i})\right),

for
j=0,\ldots,n
. Similarly to previous theorems, we will pass from the
Harish-Chandra integral of
A
to the
k
-th moment of the average empirical
distribution of
A/N
using the differential operator
\mathcal{D}_{k}
.
We have that
\mathbb{E}(\operatorname{Tr}(A^{k}))=\mathcal{D}_{k}f_{N}|_{x_{i}=0}
and

\mathcal{D}_{k}f_{N}=\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\sum_{\underset{l_{i}\neq l_{j}\text{ for }i\neq j}{l_{0},\ldots,l_{m}=1}}^{N}\sum_{b_{0}+\cdots+b_{n}=k-m}\binom{k}{m}\frac{(k-m)!}{b_{0}!\ldots b_{n}!}\frac{\partial_{l_{0}}^{b_{0}}f_{0}\partial_{l_{0}}^{b_{1}}f_{1}\ldots\partial^{b_{n}}_{l_{0}}f_{n}}{(x_{l_{0}}-x_{l_{1}})\ldots(x_{l_{0}}-x_{l_{m}})}.

(145)

As a consequence,
\mathcal{D}_{k}f_{N}
is a linear combination of terms

\text{{\hskip 30.00005pt}}f_{N}\frac{\prod_{i=0}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{b_{i}}(\Psi_{i}^{(j)}(x_{l_{0}}))^{\lambda_{i,j}}}{(x_{l_{0}}-x_{l_{1}})\ldots(x_{l_{0}}-x_{l_{m}})}+f_{N}\frac{\prod_{i=0}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{b_{i}}(\Psi_{i}^{(j)}(x_{l_{1}}))^{\lambda_{i,j}}}{(x_{l_{1}}-x_{l_{0}})(x_{l_{1}}-x_{l_{2}})\ldots(x_{l_{1}}-x_{l_{m}})}+\ldots+f_{N}\frac{\prod_{i=0}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{b_{i}}(\Psi_{i}^{(j)}(x_{l_{m}}))^{\lambda_{i,j}}}{(x_{l_{m}}-x_{l_{0}})\ldots(x_{l_{m}}-x_{l_{m-1}})},

(146)

where these terms have a limit for
x_{1}=\cdots=x_{N}=0
, due to Lemma
3
.
Note that all the coefficients in
\mathcal{D}_{k}f_{N}|_{x_{i}=0}
will
be of order
O(N^{k+1})
. In order to prove the claim, for
x_{1}=\cdots=x_{N}=0
we have to compute the terms (
146
), where their coefficients are
not
\omicron(N^{k+1-n\varepsilon})
. Since
0<\varepsilon<n^{-1}
and
b_{0}+\cdots+b_{n}=k-m
, note that
differentiating the functions
f_{0},\ldots,f_{n}
in the expression

\binom{k}{m}\frac{(k-m)!}{b_{0}!\ldots b_{n}!}\frac{\partial_{l_{0}}^{b_{0}}f_{0}\partial_{l_{0}}^{b_{1}}f_{1}\ldots\partial_{l_{0}}^{b_{n}}f_{n}}{(x_{l_{0}}-x_{l_{1}})\ldots(x_{l_{0}}-x_{l_{m}})}

can be written as a sum, where only the summand

\frac{k!}{m!b_{0}!\ldots b_{n}!}N^{b_{0}+b_{1}(1-\varepsilon)+\cdots+b_{n}(1-n\varepsilon)}f_{N}\frac{(\Psi^{\prime}_{0}(x_{l_{0}}))^{b_{0}}\ldots(\Psi^{\prime}_{n}(x_{l_{0}}))^{b_{n}}}{(x_{l_{0}}-x_{l_{1}})\ldots(x_{l_{0}}-x_{l_{m}})}

may contribute to a term (
146
) with a coefficient that is not

\omicron(N^{k+1-n\varepsilon})
. Indeed, it follows from the equality

\lim_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\rightarrow 0}\left(\frac{(\Psi^{\prime}_{0}(x_{l_{0}}))^{b_{0}}\ldots(\Psi^{\prime}_{n}(x_{l_{0}}))^{b_{n}}}{(x_{l_{0}}-x_{l_{1}})\ldots(x_{l_{0}}-x_{l_{m}})}+\frac{(\Psi^{\prime}_{0}(x_{l_{1}}))^{b_{0}}\ldots(\Psi^{\prime}_{n}(x_{l_{1}}))^{b_{n}}}{(x_{l_{1}}-x_{l_{0}})(x_{l_{1}}-x_{l_{2}})\ldots(x_{l_{1}}-x_{l_{m}})}+\right.\text{{\hskip 80.00012pt}}

\text{{\hskip 80.00012pt}}\left.\ldots+\frac{(\Psi^{\prime}_{0}(x_{l_{m}}))^{b_{0}}\ldots(\Psi^{\prime}_{n}(x_{l_{m}}))^{b_{n}}}{(x_{l_{m}}-x_{l_{0}})\ldots(x_{l_{m}}-x_{l_{m-1}})}\right)=\frac{1}{m!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}_{0}(x))^{b_{0}}\ldots(\Psi^{\prime}_{n}(x))^{b_{n}})\right|_{x=0},

in which the right hand side does not depend on
l_{0},\ldots,l_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
. Thus, the above limit appears in the right hand side of (
145
) exactly
m!\binom{N}{m+1}
times. Therefore, the summands (
146
) of

\mathcal{D}_{k}f_{N}|_{x_{i}=0}
with a coefficient that it is not
\omicron(N^{k+1-n\varepsilon})
have the form

\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!b_{0}!\ldots b_{n}!}N^{k+1-b_{1}\varepsilon-\cdots-n\text{}b_{n}\varepsilon}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}_{0}(x))^{b_{0}}\ldots(\Psi^{\prime}_{n}(x))^{b_{n}})\right|_{x=0},

(147)

where
b_{0}=k-m-b_{1}-\cdots-b_{n}
and
b_{1}+2b_{2}+\cdots+n\text{}b_{n}\leq n
. Thus, the summands that we are interested in have a factor
N^{k+1-j\varepsilon}
, for
j=0,\ldots,n
. Taking the sum of those of the form
(
147
) with the same factor
N^{k+1-j\varepsilon}
(i.e., for

b_{1}+2b_{2}+\cdots+jb_{j}=j
,
b_{j+1}=\cdots=b_{n}=0
, and
m=0,\ldots,k-b_{1}-\cdots-b_{j}
), we get that

\frac{1}{N^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}(A^{k})]=\frac{1}{N^{k+1}}\mathcal{D}_{k}(f_{0}\ldots f_{n})|_{x_{i}=0}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{(0)}+\frac{1}{N^{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{(1)}+\cdots+\frac{1}{N^{n\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{(n)}+o(N^{-n\varepsilon}),

(148)

and the claim holds.

Now we deal with a more general case, in which only relation (
141
) is
known for
A
. Again, we will use the differential operator

\mathcal{D}_{k}
in order to pass from the Harish-Chandra integral of
A
to
the
k
-th moment of the average empirical distribution of
A/N
. Taking
into account (
141
), it will be useful for our analysis to write the
average Harish-Chandra integral as
f_{N}=F_{N}\cdot G_{N}
,
where
F_{N}:=\exp(N\cdot\widetilde{F_{N}})
, where

\widetilde{F_{N}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}):=\frac{1}{N}\log\text{}f_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})-\sum_{j=0}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{N^{j\varepsilon}}\Psi_{j}(x_{i})

and

G_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}):=\prod_{j=0}^{n}\exp\left(N^{1-j\varepsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Psi_{j}(x_{i})\right).

We write
G_{N}=f_{0}f_{1}\ldots f_{n}
, where
f_{j}:=\exp(N^{1-j\varepsilon}g_{j})
and
g_{j}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}):=\Psi_{j}(x_{1})+\cdots+\Psi_{j}(x_{N})
, for every
j=0,\ldots,n
. Then, applying the operator

\mathcal{D}_{k}
to
f_{N}
, by Proposition
1
and Lemma
1
we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}^{k}(A)\right)H\text{}C(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\lambda_{1}(A),\ldots,\lambda_{N}(A))\right]

=\left.\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\sum_{\underset{l_{i}\neq l_{j}\text{ for }i\neq j}{l_{0},\ldots,l_{m}=1}}^{N}\sum_{\nu=0}^{k-m}\sum_{b_{0}+\cdots+b_{n}=k-m-\nu}\binom{k}{m}\binom{k-m}{\nu}\frac{(k-m-\nu)!}{b_{0}!\ldots b_{n}!}\frac{\partial_{l_{0}}^{\nu}F_{N}\partial_{l_{0}}^{b_{0}}f_{0}\ldots\partial_{l_{0}}^{b_{n}}f_{n}}{(x_{l_{0}}-x_{l_{1}})\ldots(x_{l_{0}}-x_{l_{m}})}\right|_{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{N}=0}.

(149)

Using the chain rule, we obtain
that
\partial_{l_{0}}^{\nu}F_{N},\partial_{l_{0}}^{b_{0}}f_{0},\ldots,\partial_{l_{0}}^{b_{n}}f_{n}
can be written as a linear combination of products
of factors
F_{N},f_{0},\ldots,f_{n}
and derivatives of
\widetilde{F_{N}},g_{0},\ldots,g_{n}
with respect to
x_{l_{0}}
, where the
coefficients depend on
N
. Writing in such a way
\partial_{l_{0}}^{\nu}F_{N}\partial_{l_{0}}^{b_{0}}f_{0}\ldots\partial_{l_{0}}^{b_{n}}f_{n}
as a sum, we will
show that the contribution of each summand to (
149
) is equal to
zero, for
\nu>0
, in the limit
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\rightarrow 0
and
N\rightarrow\infty
. We consider the summands

N^{\nu}F_{N}(\partial_{l_{0}}\widetilde{F_{N}})^{\nu},N^{b_{0}}f_{0}(\partial_{l_{0}}g_{0})^{b_{0}},N^{b_{1}(1-\varepsilon)}f_{1}(\partial_{l_{0}}g_{1})^{b_{1}},\ldots,N^{b_{n}(1-n\varepsilon)}f_{n}(\partial_{l_{0}}g_{n})^{b_{n}}

of

\partial_{l_{0}}^{\nu}F_{N},\partial_{l_{0}}^{b_{0}}f_{0},\ldots,\partial_{l_{0}}^{b_{n}}f_{n}.

We will show that that the terms

f_{N}\frac{(\partial_{l_{0}}\widetilde{F_{N}})^{\nu}(\partial_{l_{0}}g_{0})^{b_{0}}\ldots(\partial_{l_{0}}g_{n})^{b_{n}}}{(x_{l_{0}}-x_{l_{1}})\ldots(x_{l_{0}}-x_{l_{m}})}+f_{N}\frac{(\partial_{l_{1}}\widetilde{F_{N}})^{\nu}(\partial_{l_{1}}g_{0})^{b_{0}}\ldots(\partial_{l_{1}}g_{n})^{b_{n}}}{(x_{l_{1}}-x_{l_{0}})(x_{l_{1}}-x_{l_{2}})\ldots(x_{l_{1}}-x_{l_{m}})}+

\text{\qquad}\ldots+f_{N}\frac{(\partial_{l_{m}}\widetilde{F_{N}})^{\nu}(\partial_{l_{m}}g_{0})^{b_{0}}\ldots(\partial_{l_{m}}g_{n})^{b_{n}}}{(x_{l_{m}}-x_{l_{0}})\ldots(x_{l_{m}}-x_{l_{m-1}})}

(150)

that contribute to (
149
) can be controlled when
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\rightarrow 0
. In order to do so, we can use a similar
to the proof of Theorem
8
argument. The first step is to consider
x_{i}=i\varepsilon
, for every
i=1,\ldots,N
. For every
i=1,\ldots,N
, we consider the
functions

h_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}):=[(\partial_{i}\widetilde{F_{N}})^{\nu}(\partial_{i}g_{0})^{b_{0}}\ldots(\partial_{i}g_{n})^{b_{n}}](x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})

and
H_{i}(\varepsilon):=h_{i}(\varepsilon,2\varepsilon,\ldots,N\varepsilon)
. Then, if we consider for every
i=1,\ldots,N
the Taylor
expansion

H_{i}(\varepsilon)=H_{i}(0)+H^{\prime}_{i}(0)\varepsilon+\cdots+\frac{\varepsilon^{m}}{m!}H_{i}^{(m)}(0)+\varepsilon^{m}\widetilde{H_{i}}(0),

where
\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\widetilde{H_{i}}(\varepsilon)=0
,
we can control (
150
) as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, because

\frac{H_{l_{0}}^{(l)}(0)}{(l_{0}-l_{1})\ldots(l_{0}-l_{m})}+\frac{H_{l_{1}}^{(l)}(0)}{(l_{1}-l_{0})(l_{1}-l_{2})\ldots(l_{1}-l_{m})}+\cdots+\frac{H_{l_{m}}^{(l)}(0)}{(l_{m}-l_{0})\ldots(l_{m}-l_{m-1})}=0,

for
l<m
. Indeed, one has

H_{i}^{(l)}(0)=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}=1}^{N}i_{1}\ldots i_{l}\partial_{i_{l}}\ldots\partial_{i_{1}}h_{i}(0^{N}),

and since the functions
\widetilde{F_{N}},g_{0},\ldots,g_{n}
are symmetric
for two
(l+1)
-tuples
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{l+1})
and
(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{l+1})
of elements of
\{1,\ldots,N\}
, we have

\partial_{\alpha_{1}}\ldots\partial_{\alpha_{l}}h_{\alpha_{l+1}}(0^{N})=\partial_{\beta_{1}}\ldots\partial_{\beta_{l}}h_{\beta_{l+1}}(0^{N}),

where
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{j}\Leftrightarrow\beta_{i}=\beta_{j}
, for every
i,j
. Thus,
H_{i}^{(l)}(0)
is a polynomial in
i
of degree
l
and in the limit

\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
the expression (
150
) is equal to a linear
combination of derivatives

\partial_{i_{m}}\ldots\partial_{i_{1}}[(\partial_{i}\widetilde{F_{N}})^{\nu}(\partial_{i}g_{0})^{b_{0}}\ldots(\partial_{i}g_{n})^{b_{n}}](0^{N}),

where
i,i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,m+1\}
, and the coefficients
do not depend on
N
. This shows that as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
,
the expression (
150
) does not depend on
l_{0},\ldots,l_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
. Therefore, the above linear combinations of derivatives will
appear
m!\binom{N}{m+1}
times in (
149
), as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. Thus, the summands

N^{\nu+b_{0}+b_{1}(1-\varepsilon)+\cdots+b_{n}(1-n\varepsilon)}f_{N}(\partial_{l_{0}}\widetilde{F_{N}})^{\nu}(\partial_{l_{0}}g_{0})^{b_{0}}\ldots(\partial_{l_{0}}g_{n})^{b_{n}}

(151)

of
\partial_{l_{0}}^{\nu}F_{N}\partial_{l_{0}}^{b_{0}}f_{0}\ldots\partial_{l_{0}}^{b_{n}}f_{n}
will contribute to (
149
) by a factor

N^{n\varepsilon-(k+1)}
, as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, with a linear
combination of terms

\left(\frac{N^{n\varepsilon}}{N^{\varepsilon b_{1}+2\varepsilon b_{2}+\cdots+n\varepsilon b_{n}}}+\omicron(1)\right)\partial_{i_{m}}\ldots\partial_{i_{1}}[(\partial_{i}\widetilde{F_{N}})^{\nu}(\partial_{i}g_{0})^{b_{0}}\ldots(\partial_{i}g_{n})^{b_{n}}](0^{N}),

for
b_{0}+\cdots+b_{n}=k-m-\nu
and
0<\varepsilon<n^{-1}
.
For
\nu>0
, due to (
141
), we have

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{n\varepsilon}\partial_{i_{l}}\ldots\partial_{i_{1}}\widetilde{F_{N}}(0^{N})=0,

for all
l
, and we obtain that these linear combinations will not
contribute to
N^{n\varepsilon-(k+1)}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}(A^{k})]
, as

N\rightarrow\infty
. Let
\psi_{l_{0}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
be one of the
remaining summands of
\partial_{l_{0}}^{\nu}F_{N}\partial_{l_{0}}^{b_{0}}f_{0}\ldots\partial_{l_{0}}^{b_{n}}f_{n}
, where they are products and their factors
that depend from
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
are
f_{N}
and derivarives of

\widetilde{F_{N}},g_{0},\ldots,g_{n}
with respect to
x_{l_{0}}
. Then, we can
use the same argument to control the terms

\frac{\psi_{l_{0}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})}{(x_{l_{0}}-x_{l_{1}})\ldots(x_{l_{0}}-x_{l_{m}})}+\cdots+\frac{\psi_{l_{m}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})}{(x_{l_{m}}-x_{l_{0}})\ldots(x_{l_{m}}-x_{l_{m-1}})}

(152)

that contribute to (
149
), when
x_{i}=i\varepsilon
and

\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. Similarly to the case that we analyzed above,
where
\psi_{l_{0}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
has a form (
151
), for the same reasons
(
152
) will converge to a linear combination of products of
derivatives of
\widetilde{F_{N}},g_{0},\ldots,g_{n}
at
0^{N}
. For
\nu>0
,
we have that each of its summands will have as factor a derivative of

\widetilde{F_{N}}
at
0^{N}
. Taking into account that as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, (
152
) does not depend on
l_{0},\ldots,l_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and that the factor of
\psi_{l_{0}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})

that does not depend on
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
is
O(N^{k-m-1})
, we obtain
that this factor multiplied by
N^{n\varepsilon-(k+1)}\binom{N}{m+1}
will converge to zero as
N\rightarrow\infty
. Moreover, since all
the derivatives of
\widetilde{F_{N}}
at
0^{N}
converge (to zero), due to
(
141
), the expression (
152
) will not contribute to
N^{n\varepsilon-(k+1)}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}(A^{k})]
, as
N\rightarrow\infty
. Thus,
considering the case
\nu=0
in (
149
), as
N\rightarrow\infty
, we obtain
that

\frac{N^{n\varepsilon}}{N^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}(A^{k})]=\frac{N^{n\varepsilon}}{N^{k+1}}\mathcal{D}_{k}G_{N}|_{x_{i}=0}+\omicron(1).

But since in (
148
) we proved that

\frac{1}{N^{k+1}}\mathcal{D}_{k}G_{N}|_{x_{i}=0}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{(0)}+\frac{1}{N^{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{(1)}+\cdots+\frac{1}{N^{n\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{(n)}+\omicron(N^{-n\varepsilon}),

we arrive at the claim of the theorem.
∎

Remark 6

In the context of Theorem
17
, note that for
\Psi:=\Psi_{0},\Phi:=\Psi_{1}
and
\mathrm{T}:=\Psi_{2}
, we have that

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{(0)}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{(1)}=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{k}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(2)}_{k}:=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}
,
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{k},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}
were defined in (
54
), (
81
), (
126
),
repsectively. Thus, for the average empirical distribution functional of
A/N
we have, for every
P\in\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle
, that

\varphi_{N}(P)=\varphi(P)+\frac{1}{N^{\varepsilon}}\varphi^{\prime}(P)+\frac{1}{N^{2\varepsilon}}\frac{\varphi^{\prime\prime}(P)}{2}+\omicron(N^{-2\varepsilon}),

where
(\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle,\varphi,\varphi^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime\prime})
is the infinitesimal non-commutative probability space of order

2
. Hence we see that compared to Theorem
16
, the
limit regime (
141
) for the logarithm of the Harish-Chandra integral
of
A
gives an expansion for
\varphi_{N}
, where the rate of convergence
that gives the correction to the non-commutative distribution of
A
is

N^{\varepsilon}
instead of
N
. As a corollary, the functional

\varphi^{\prime\prime}
determines completely the second order correction since the term
(
127
) that contributes to the limit regime (
125
) will not
affect the
1/N^{2\varepsilon}
correction. This holds because assumption
(
141
) implies the expansion (
140
) and the
1/N^{n\varepsilon}
correction (
142
) of
A/N
will be given by

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{n\varepsilon}\left(M_{0,N}(\Psi)-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{(0)}-\frac{1}{N^{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{(1)}-\cdots-\frac{1}{N^{(n-1)\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{(n-1)}\right),

(153)

since
0<\varepsilon<n^{-1}
. On the other hand, assuming the limit
regime (
141
) for
A
with
\varepsilon=1
, in order to compute the

1/N^{n}
correction, besides of (
153
)
there are extra limits that we have to consider. For example, for
n=3
,
the limits

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N(M_{2,N}(\Psi)-\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}),\text{\quad}\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}M_{3,N}(\Psi).

will also contribute non-trivially to the
1/N^{3}
correction

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{3}\left(\frac{1}{N^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}(A^{k})]-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}-\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{k}-\frac{1}{N^{2}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime\prime}_{k}+\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime\prime}_{k})\right).

Note also that for
n=1
the
first order fluctuations that we get from the limit regime of Theorem
12

coincide with those of the
\varepsilon<1
regime because
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}M_{1,N}(\Psi)=0
.

Remark 7

Theorem
17
(for
n=1
) and Theorem
12
give the
same formula for the
1/N^{\varepsilon}
and the
1/N
correction,
respectively. However, this is not true for the second order correction, if we
compare Theorem
17
(for
n=2
) and Theorem
16
.
A (technical) reason why this holds for
the first order corrections is that in the limit regime

\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{E}[H\text{}C(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},0^{N-r};\lambda_{1}(A),\ldots,\lambda_{N}(A))]\longrightarrow\Psi(x_{1})+\cdots+\Psi(x_{r}),

(154)

applying the differential operator to the Harish-Chandra transform, one obtains (see the proofs of the theorems) an
asymptotic expansion

\frac{1}{N^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}(A^{k})]=\sum_{i=0}^{k}M_{i,k,N}(\Psi)\frac{1}{N^{i}},

(155)

where
M_{1,k,N}(\Psi)=0
. Therefore
M_{1,k,N}(\Psi)
does not
contribute to the
1/N
correction of Theorem
12
. On the other hand the extra
term
\nu^{\prime\prime}
of Theorem
16
, is the
N\rightarrow\infty
limit of {
M_{2,k,N}(\Psi)
}.

Using similar arguments, one can check that
M_{3,k,N}(\Psi)
=0. More
generally, it is believed by the authors that in the asymptotic expansion
(
155
) all the odd terms vanish. Therefore, (
154
) leads to an asymptotic
expansion of a similar form as in, e.g.,
[
Par
]
.

Remark 8

One source of examples of random matrix models that satisfy the limit regime (
141
)
are sums of ergodic unitarily invariant matrices. In more detail, for
functions
\Psi_{0},\ldots,\Psi_{n}
of the form (
32
) and
0<\varepsilon<n^{-1}
, we consider
n
independent Hermitian random matrices
A_{0},\ldots,A_{n}
of size
N
, such that

\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Tr}(H\text{}A_{j})]=\prod_{b\in\operatorname{Spec}(H)}\exp(N^{1-j\varepsilon}\Psi_{j}(b)),\text{\quad for every }H\in H(N),

and
j=0,\ldots,n
. Then
A:=A_{0}+\cdots+A_{n}
satisfy the
relation (
144
). Note that the formula (
143
) for
the
1/N^{j\varepsilon}
correction of
A/N
, for
j=0,\ldots,n
,
implies that the matrices
A_{j+1},\ldots,A_{n}
do not affect the
1/N^{i\varepsilon}
correction of
A/N
, for
i=0,\ldots,j
.

Our next result provides an interpretation of the moment formulas (
143
) via infinitesimal free probability of order
n
.

Theorem 18

Let
\Psi_{0},\ldots,\Psi_{n}
be infinitely differentiable functions at
0

and consider the sequences
(c_{m}^{(0)})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\ldots
,

(c^{(n)}_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
, where for every
i=0,\ldots,n
,

c_{m}^{(i)}:=\frac{i!\Psi_{i}^{(m)}(0)}{(m-1)!},\text{\hskip 10.22217ptfor
every }m\in\mathbb{N}.

Then, for the sequence
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{(n)})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
defined
in (
143
) we have, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the equality

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{(n)}=\sum_{\underset{\pi=\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{l}\}}{\pi\in\operatorname{NC}(k)}}\sum_{\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{l}=n}\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}!\ldots\lambda_{l}!}c_{|V_{1}|}^{(\lambda_{1})}\ldots c_{|V_{l}|}^{(\lambda_{l})}.

(156)

Proof.

In order to prove the claim, we describe how the indices
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n},m
from the sum

\sum_{b_{1}+2b_{2}+\cdots+n\text{}b_{n}=n}\sum_{m=0}^{k-b_{1}-\cdots-b_{n}}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-b_{1}-\cdots-b_{n})!b_{1}!\ldots b_{n}!}\text{{\hskip 80.00012pt}}

\text{{\hskip 110.00017pt}}\times\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}_{0}(x))^{k-m-b_{1}-\cdots-b_{n}}(\Psi^{\prime}_{1}(x))^{b_{1}}\ldots(\Psi^{\prime}_{n}(x))^{b_{n}})\right|_{x=0}

(157)

can be matched with those from the right hand side of
(
156
). Note that for

\pi=\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{k-m}\}\in\operatorname{NC}(k)
, with
m=0,\ldots,k-1
, the sum

\sum_{\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{k-m}=n}\frac{n!}{\lambda_{1}!\ldots\lambda_{k-m}!}c_{|V_{1}|}^{(\lambda_{1})}\ldots c_{|V_{k-m}|}^{(\lambda_{k-m})}

(158)

looks like a formal
n
-th derivative of the product
c^{(0)}_{|V_{1}|}\ldots c^{(0)}_{|V_{k-m}|}
, if we view
c_{l}^{(i)}
as a formal
i
-th
derivative of
c_{l}^{(0)}
. For fixed
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}
with
b_{1}+2b_{2}+\cdots+n\text{}b_{n}=n
and
m\in\{0,\ldots,k-b_{1}-\cdots-b_{n}\}
, let
b_{0}:=k-m-b_{1}-\cdots-b_{n}
. Then, by Leibniz
rule,

\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}((\Psi^{\prime}_{0}(x))^{b_{0}}\ldots(\Psi^{\prime}_{n}(x))^{b_{n}})\right|_{x=0}=\sum\frac{m!}{\prod_{i=1}^{b_{0}}l_{i,0}!\ldots\prod_{i=1}^{b_{n}}l_{i,n}!}\prod_{i=1}^{b_{0}}\Psi_{0}^{(l_{i,0}+1)}(0)\ldots\prod_{i=1}^{b_{n}}\Psi_{n}^{(l_{i,n}+1)}(0)

\text{{\hskip 100.00015pt}}=\sum m!\left(\prod_{i=0}^{n}(i!)^{b_{i}}\right)^{-1}\prod_{i=1}^{b_{0}}c^{(0)}_{l_{i,0}+1}\ldots\prod_{i=1}^{b_{n}}c^{(n)}_{l_{i,n}+1},

(159)

where the sum is over all tuples of non-negative integers
((l_{i,0})_{i=1}^{b_{0}},\ldots,(l_{i,n})_{i=1}^{b_{n}})
satisfying the constraint

\sum_{i=1}^{b_{0}}l_{i,0}+\cdots+\sum_{i=1}^{b_{n}}l_{i,n}=m.

Thus, in order to pass from (
157
) to (
156
),
we will provide a correspondence between summands of
(
159
) and summands of (
158
),
where we differentiate one time
b_{1}
factors, two times
b_{2}

factors,…,
n
times
b_{n}
factors of
c^{(0)}_{|V_{1}|}\ldots c^{(0)}_{|V_{k-m}|}
, i.e.,

|\{i=1,\ldots,k-m:\lambda_{i}=j\}|=b_{j},

for every
j=1,\ldots,n
. For this reason we need to have an equality
m\leq k-b_{1}-\cdots-b_{n}
, since such a partition must have at least
b_{1}+\cdots+b_{n}
blocks. On the other hand, the integers
l_{i,j}+1
will express the
cardinalities of the blocks of
\pi
. We consider a tuple
((\lambda_{i,0})_{i=1}^{b_{0}},\ldots,(\lambda_{i,n})_{i=1}^{b_{n}})
such that their sum
is equal to
m
. Previously, in the cases
n=0,1,2
, we proved the claim by showing
that the contribution of all
\sigma=\{W_{1},\ldots,W_{k-m}\}\in\operatorname{NC}(k)
that have the same number of blocks with
i
elements, for
every
i=1,\ldots,k
, to the right hand side of (
156
), is
equal to the contribution of
(|W_{1}|-1,\ldots,|W_{k-m}|-1)
to

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{(n)}
. For arbitrary
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we will show that the contribution of

\prod_{i=1}^{b_{0}}c_{\lambda_{i,0}+1}^{(0)}\ldots\prod_{i=1}^{b_{n}}c_{\lambda_{i,n}+1}^{(n)}

(160)

to
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{(n)}
and to the right hand side of (
156
)
will be the same. This suffices to prove the claim. For this purpose, for
j=0,\ldots,n
, we assume that
\{\lambda_{1,j},\ldots,\lambda_{b_{j},j}\}=\{\mu_{1,j},\ldots,\mu_{a_{j},j}\}
, where
\mu_{p,j}\neq\mu_{q,j}
for every
p\neq q
. We also define

r_{i,j}:=|\{p=1,\ldots,b_{j}:\lambda_{p,j}=\mu_{i,j}\}|,

for every
i=1,\ldots,a_{j}
and
j=0,\ldots,n
. Then, one has

\sum_{i=1}^{a_{j}}r_{i,j}=b_{j}\text{\quad and\quad}\sum_{i=1}^{a_{j}}r_{i,j}\mu_{i,j}=\sum_{i=1}^{b_{j}}\lambda_{i,j}.

Therefore, the number of different ways for

\prod_{i=1}^{b_{j}}c_{\lambda_{i,j}+1}^{(j)}=\prod_{i=1}^{a_{j}}(c_{\mu_{i,j}+1}^{(j)})^{r_{i,j}}

to become a factor of a summand of (
159
) is equal
to the number of different ways that we can cover
b_{j}
points on a line
segment with
r_{1,j}
number of elements
\mu_{1,j}
, …,

r_{a_{j},j}
number of elements
\mu_{a_{j},j}
. This is equal to
(\prod_{i=1}^{a_{j}}r_{i,j}!)^{-1}b_{j}!
, and it implies that
(
160
) will contribute

\left((m+1)!\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{n}\prod_{i=1}^{a_{j}}r_{i,j}!\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{n}(i!)^{b_{i}}\right)^{-1}k!

times to
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{(n)}
.

Now let us analyze the right hand side of
(
156
). Its summands with factor (
160
)
correspond to non-crossing partitions
\{\{V_{i,0}\}_{i=1}^{b_{0}},\ldots,\{V_{i,n}\}_{i=1}^{b_{n}}\}\in\operatorname{NC}(k)
such that
|V_{i,j}|=\lambda_{i,j}+1
, for every
i=1,\ldots,b_{j}
and
j=0,\ldots,n
. For such partitions the corresponding products

\prod_{i=1}^{b_{0}}c_{|V_{i,0}|}^{(0)}\ldots\prod_{i=1}^{b_{n}}c_{|V_{i,n}|}^{(0)}

(161)

have the same formal
n
-th derivative. We assume that such a partition has

r_{1}
blocks with
\mu_{1}+1
elements,…,
r_{a}
blocks with
\mu_{a}+1
elements. Then

r_{1}+\cdots+r_{a}=k-m\text{\quad and\quad}r_{1}(\mu_{1}+1)+\cdots+r_{a}(\mu_{a}+1)=k.

These
r_{i}
’s depend on whether some of the
\mu_{i,j}
’s are equal to each other. By
definition, for every
p=1,\ldots,a
,
r_{p}
is a finite sum of
r_{i,j}
’s and every
r_{i,j}
will be a summand of a unique
r_{p}
. The
r_{i,j}
will be a summand of
r_{p}
if and only if
\mu_{i,j}=\mu_{p}
. Thus,
for every
p=1,\ldots,a
, one has

r_{p}=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{a_{j}}r_{i,j}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{\mu_{i,j}=\mu_{p}\}}.

There are
((m+1)!\prod_{i=1}^{a}r_{i}!)^{-1}k!
such non-crossing
partitions. Moreover, for such partitions we are interested in summands inside

\sum\frac{1}{\prod_{i=1}^{r_{1}}l_{i,1}!\ldots\prod_{i=1}^{r_{a}}l_{i,a}!}\prod_{i=1}^{r_{1}}c_{\mu_{1}+1}^{(l_{i,1})}\ldots\prod_{i=1}^{r_{a}}c_{\mu_{a}+1}^{(l_{i,a})}

(162)

which have factor (
160
). The above sum is over all tuples
of nonnegative integers
((l_{i,1})_{i=1}^{r_{1}},\ldots,(l_{i,a})_{i=1}^{r_{a}})
, such that their sum is equal to
n
. These terms appear when the
l_{i,j}
’s have been chosen in order to get a product where we differentiate one
time
b_{1}
terms
c_{\mu_{i}+1}^{(0)}
, …,
n
times
b_{n}
terms

c_{\mu_{i}+1}^{(0)}
. Thus, for these summands, the product of the
corresponding
l_{i,j}!
is equal to
\prod_{i=0}^{n}(i!)^{b_{i}}
.
We have to find the number of ways we have to choose the
l_{i,j}
’s in
order to get such a product with factor (
160
). For this
purpose, for some
p\in\{1,\ldots,a\}
, we assume that
r_{p}=r_{i_{1},j_{1}}+\cdots+r_{i_{l},j_{l}}
, where
0\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{l}\leq n
.
This implies that
\mu_{i_{1},j_{1}}=\cdots=\mu_{i_{l},j_{l}}=\mu_{p}
. Therefore,
the number of different ways for

\left(c_{\mu_{i_{1},j_{1}}+1}^{(j_{1})}\right)^{r_{i_{1},j_{1}}}\ldots\left(c_{\mu_{i_{l},j_{l}}+1}^{(j_{l})}\right)^{r_{i_{l},j_{l}}}

to become a factor of a summand of (
162
) is equal to the number
of different ways that we can cover
r_{p}
points on a line segment with

r_{i_{1},j_{1}}
number of elements
j_{1}
, …,
r_{i_{l},j_{l}}
number
of elements
j_{l}
. This is equal to
(r_{i_{1},j_{1}}!\ldots r_{i_{l},j_{l}}!)^{-1}r_{p}!
. Therefore, we deduce that the term (
160
)
contributes

\frac{k!}{(m+1)!\prod_{i=1}^{a}r_{i}!}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{a}r_{i}!}{\prod_{j=0}^{n}\prod_{i=1}^{a_{j}}r_{i,j}!\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{n}(i!)^{b_{i}}}

times to the right hand side of (
156
). This proves the claim.
∎

Remark 9

Theorem
17
and Theorem
18
show that the limit regime
(
141
) for a random Hermitian matrix
A
provides us a version of Taylor
expansion for the average empirical distribution functional of
A/N
. An
infinitesimal non-commutative probability space of higher order plays the
role of the derivatives. More precisely, for the functional

\varphi_{N}:\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
,
defined in (
87
), we have the expansion

\varphi_{N}(P)=\varphi^{(0)}(P)+\frac{1}{N^{\varepsilon}}\varphi^{(1)}(P)+\cdots+\frac{1}{N^{n\varepsilon}}\frac{\varphi^{(n)}(P)}{n!}+\omicron(N^{-n\varepsilon}),\text{\quad for every }P\in\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle,

(163)

where
(\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle,\varphi^{(0)},\ldots,\varphi^{(n)})
is an infinitesimal non-commutative probability space of
order
n
with infinitesimal non-crossing cumulant functionals given by

(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{(0)}_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}},\ldots,(\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{m}^{(n)})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
, where

\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{m}^{(i)}(\mathbf{x},\ldots,\mathbf{x})=\frac{i!\Psi_{i}^{(m)}(0)}{(m-1)!},

for every
i=0,\ldots,n
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Example 10

We compute the corresponding infinitesimal non-commutative probability space

(\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle,\varphi^{(0)},\varphi^{(1)},\varphi^{(2)},\varphi^{(3)}),

described in (
163
), for the random
matrix
A=N\cdot A_{1}+N^{1-\varepsilon/2}A_{2}+N^{1-\varepsilon}A_{3}+N^{1-3\varepsilon/2}A_{4}
, where
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},A_{4}
are
independent GUE matrices. Then
\varphi_{0}
is given by the semicircle distribution
and the linear functionals
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
have been computed in
Example
2
and Example
8
, respectively.
Theorem
17
implies that the values
\varphi_{3}(\mathbf{x}^{k})
are
equal to

\sum_{m=0}^{k-3}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-3)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}(x^{k-m})\right|_{x=0}+6\sum_{m=0}^{k-2}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-2)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}(x^{k-m})\right|_{x=0}

+6\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{k!}{m!(m+1)!(k-m-1)!}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}x^{m}}(x^{k-m})\right|_{x=0}.

Using the Cauchy formula, we see that this is equal to

\frac{k(k-1)}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}z^{3}\left(z+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k-2}d\text{}z+\frac{3k}{\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}z^{2}\left(z+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k-1}d\text{}z+\frac{3}{\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}z\left(z+\frac{1}{z}\right)^{k}d\text{}z.

This implies that the linear functional
\varphi^{(3)}
is given by

\varphi^{(3)}(P)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}\left(\frac{t^{4}-4t^{2}+2}{\sqrt{4-t^{2}}}P^{\prime\prime}(t)+\frac{6t^{3}-18t}{\sqrt{4-t^{2}}}P^{\prime}(t)+\frac{6t^{2}-12}{\sqrt{4-t^{2}}}P(t)\right)d\text{}t,

for every
P\in\mathbb{C}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle
.

Example 11

We consider the random matrix

A=N\cdot G+\theta_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor N^{1-\varepsilon}\rfloor}X_{i}X_{i}^{\ast}+\theta_{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor N^{1-2\varepsilon}\rfloor}Y_{i}Y_{i}^{\ast},

where the matrices
G,\{X_{i}\},\{Y_{i}\}
are independent,
G
is a GUE of
size
N
and
X_{i},Y_{i}
are independent standard complex Gaussian
N\times 1
vectors.

Then, in notation of Theorem
17
, we have that

\Psi_{0}(x)=\frac{x^{2}}{2}\text{, \qquad}\Psi_{1}(x)=\log(1-\theta_{1}x)^{-1}\text{, \qquad}\Psi_{2}(x)=\log(1-\theta_{2}x)^{-1}.

Applying the theorem and performing similar to previous examples computations,
we arrive to the following answer.
Consider the infinitesimal non-commutative probability space of
order 2,
(\mathbb{C}\langle X\rangle,\varphi^{(0)},\varphi^{(1)},\varphi^{(2)})
, where
\varphi^{(0)}
is the limit,
\varphi^{(1)}
is the
first order correction and
\varphi^{(2)}
is the second order correction.

This implies that

\varphi^{(1)}(P)=\boldsymbol{1}_{|\theta_{1}|\geq 1}\cdot P(\theta_{1}+\theta_{1}^{-1})-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}\frac{t-2\theta_{1}}{(t-\theta_{1}-\theta_{1}^{-1})\sqrt{4-t^{2}}}P(t)d\text{}t,

and

\varphi^{(2)}(P)=\boldsymbol{1}_{|\theta_{2}|\geq 1}\cdot P(\theta_{2}+\theta_{2}^{-1})-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}\frac{t-2\theta_{2}}{(t-\theta_{2}-\theta_{2}^{-1})\sqrt{4-t^{2}}}P(t)d\text{}t\text{
{\hskip 120.00018pt}}

\text{{\hskip 60.00009pt}}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}\frac{\theta_{1}^{2}}{\sqrt{4-t^{2}}}\frac{P^{\prime}(t)-P^{\prime}(\theta_{1}+\theta^{-1}_{1})}{t-\theta_{1}-\theta_{1}^{-1}}d\text{}t\hskip 160.00024pt

\text{{\hskip 70.0001pt}}\quad-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}\frac{(\theta_{1}^{2}-1)(t-2\theta_{1})}{(t-\theta_{1}-\theta_{1}^{-1})\sqrt{4-t^{2}}}\left(\frac{P^{\prime}(t)-P^{\prime}(\theta_{1}+\theta_{1}^{-1})}{t-\theta_{1}-\theta_{1}^{-1}}-P^{\prime\prime}(\theta_{1}+\theta_{1}^{-1})\right)d\text{}t.

Note that the second correction lives on the scale
N^{1-2\varepsilon}
, for
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}
. Therefore, the presence of the delta measure in
\varphi^{(2)}(P)
means the existence of
N^{1-2\varepsilon}
outliers at
\theta_{2}+\theta_{2}^{-1}
in case
|\theta_{2}|\geq 1
. This can be viewed as a
higher order
BBP phase transition — it is not visible from the first order infinitesimal freeness (which governs
N^{1-\varepsilon}
scale), so in order to see these outliers, one needs to consider higher orders of infinitesimal freeness.
